1 INTRODUCTION
1.1

Historical remarks

Thin-walled structures such as plates and shells are the most
common construction elements in nature and technology.
This is independent of the specific scale. They might be
small, such as cell membranes and tiny machine parts, or
very large, such as cooling towers and air plane fuselages.
This preference to apply walls as thin as possible is a natural
Encyclopedia of Computational Mechanics Second Edition,
Edited by Erwin Stein, René de Borst and Thomas J.R. Hughes.
© 2017 John Wiley & Sons, Ltd.
DOI: 10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026

optimization strategy to reduce dead load and to minimize
the amount of construction material. The advantageous effect
of double curvature is utilized in shell structures to carry
transverse loading in an optimal way, namely predominantly
by membrane forces (as opposed to bending moments).
This is a classical design principle already known to the
ancient master builders. Their considerable heuristic knowledge allowed them to create remarkable buildings, such
as the Roman Pantheon (115–126) and the Hagia Sophia
(532–537) in Constantinople, both still existing today. It was
not before the Renaissance that scientists began to mathematically idealize the structural response, a process that we
denote nowadays as modeling and simulation.
Already, Leonardo da Vinci (1452–1519) stated (Codex
Madrid I) a principle that later on emerged to a beam
model. The subsequent process, associated with names
such as Galileo (1564–1642), Mariotte (1620–1684),
Leibniz (1646–1716), Jakob I Bernoulli (1654–1705),
Euler (1707–1783), Coulomb (1736–1806), and Navier
(1785–1836), led to what we call today Euler–Bernoulli
beam theory (Timoshenko, 1953; Szabó, 1979). This development was driven by the ingenious ideas to condense the
complex three-dimensional situation to the essential ingredients of structural response such as stretching, bending,
and torsion, and to cast this into a manageable mathematical
format. The inclusion of transverse shear deformation is
attributed (1859) to Bresse (1822–1883) and extended (1921)
to dynamics by Timoshenko (1878–1972), whose name has
established itself as a common denomination for this model.
Extensions to further effects such as uniform and warping
torsion, stability problems, cross-sectional distortion,
and further refinements, for example, including higher order
kinematics, follows in the nineteenth and twentieth centuries.

Models and Finite Elements for Thin-Walled Structures

The development of the theory of masonry arches and
vaults had its own history, also starting with Leonardo
da Vinci (for a detailed elaboration on this subject confer
Benvenuto, (1991)). The primary aspect in this context was
description of failure mechanisms, a problem investigated up
to the present time (see, e.g., the valuable work of Heyman).
In addition, Robert Hooke’s (1635–1703) “Riddle of the
Arch” has to be referred to, phrased in the anagram “abcccddeeeeefggiiiiiiiillmmmmnnnnnooprrsssttttttuuuuuuuvx”
resolving to the Latin phrase “Ut pendet continuum flexile,
sic stabit contiguum rigidum inversum” (literally translated:
As the flexible cable hangs, the inverted arch stands). It
constitutes a form-finding principle for arches and shells
(Mainstone, 1975), which became known as the inverted
chain and membrane principle, often applied in the sequel.
Christopher Wren’s construction of St. Paul Cathedral in
London, Poleni’s experiment for the rehabilitation of the
cupola of St. Peter in Rome, Rondelet’s French Pantheon,
Gaudi’s work in Catalonia up to modern shell designs
by Otto and Isler are based on this principle (Ramm and
Reitinger, 1992).
The history of the development of two-dimensional plate
theories has its own peculiarities (Timoshenko, 1953; Szabó,
1979). Inspired by Chladni’s (1756–1827) experiments
with vibrating plates, Jakob II Bernoulli (1759–1789)
formulated a lattice model of overlapping beams, yet
neglecting the twisting mode. This omission was corrected
by others later on. The related competition proposed by the
French Academy, Sophie Germain’s (1776–1831) various
attempts to win the prize and the involvement of Lagrange
(1736–1813) became famous. They and others, such as
Poisson (1781–1840) and Navier, derived the proper differential equation. However, they still had some shortcomings
in their result, in particular, with respect to the involved
elastic constants. Kirchhoff (1824–1887) finally removed
all doubts in his celebrated article in Kirchhoff (1850) and
he is credited as the founder of modern plate theory. It took
almost a century before E. Reissner (1913–1996) Reissner
(1944, 1945) and Mindlin (1906–1987) Mindlin (1951)
extended the model including the effects of transverse shear
deformations, which are particularly important in the context
of vibrations. Innumerable modifications and extensions,
such as anisotropy, large deformation (v. Kármán plate
theory), and higher order theories, have been derived over
the years.
It is interesting that the initial derivation of a shell formulation was also primarily motivated by vibration problems.
Euler developed in 1764 a model to simulate the tones of
bells, cutting the axisymmetric structure into rings, applying
curved beam models, and neglecting the meridional effects.
Also here, it took over a century before a consistent theory
of thin shells had been derived by Love (1888) (August E.

H. Love, 1863–1940). It is based on Kirchhoff’s method
and thus became known as the Kirchhoff–Love model. For a
description of the subsequent emergence of this shell model
and the related controversies among Love and his contemporaries on the role of the boundary conditions of both the
membrane and the bending part (in particular Lord Rayleigh
(1842–1919) and Lamb (1849–1934)), we refer to the very
interesting article by Calladine (1988). The need for detailed
solutions has driven research in the first half of the twentieth
century. Names such as H. Reissner, Meissner, Geckeler,
Flügge, Vlasov, and Novozhilov have to be mentioned, to
name just a few. Later on, further refinements have been
introduced by E. Reissner, Gol’denveizer, Koiter, Naghdi,
and many others. The inclusion of transverse shear deformations sometimes linked to the name of Naghdi today mostly is
referred to as a Reissner–Mindlin formulation, in recognition
of their extended plate theories.
The series of names in connection with shell formulations
could be continued forever. There are very few topics in
structural mechanics where so many investigations have been
published. Even for experts, it is hardly possible to have an
overall view on the number of theories and finite element
models developed so far. This is a strong indication for the
complexity of the involved mechanics on the one hand and
their practical relevance on the other hand.
It is obvious that the early developments of theories for
thin-walled structures have been governed primarily by the
main applications in those times, namely buildings in architecture. In the twentieth century, and particularly with the
emerging of computational methods, other applications, for
example, in mechanical engineering, in automotive and
aerospace industry, and in biomechanics, became dominant
and the driving force for further developments.
Large displacements, rotations and strains, buckling,
composites, material nonlinearities, coupled problems,
multiscale and multiphysics, solution methods, and higher
order formulations are a few keywords for problems that
have been extensively investigated in the past few years.
The finite element method as a discretization concept is
absolutely predominant.
An interesting example as to how the finite element
developments have influenced the selection of specific
structural models is the early shift from Kirchhoff–Love
formulations to those with Reissner–Mindlin kinematics
for plate and shell elements. It is motivated mainly by the
reduced compatibility requirements, although from the
practical point of application, the Kirchhoff–Love model is
often sufficient. Traditionally, the problem of formulating
C1 -continuous shape functions for two-dimensional problems involving arbitrarily element shapes and unstructured
meshes has been a deterrent obstacle. Recently, with the
systematic use of subdivision methods and nonuniform

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

2

rational B-splines (NURBS), particularly in the context of
the so-called isogeometric approach, Kirchhoff-Love-type
shell elements experience a renaissance. Furthermore, for
shear-deformable shell formulations, higher continuity
appears to offer remarkable advantages.
In times where computer capacity has become so powerful
and the simulation tools have reached such a high level
of sophistication, we should not forget the main driving
force of our ancestors: concentration on the essentials and
reduction to the principal mechanical behavior of thin-walled
structures (Ramm and Wall, 2004). Mechanical models are
not only a tool for analysis of structures but also for their
design, the latter requiring insight into the essential structural
behavior.

1.2

Overview

This chapter concentrates on the mathematical modeling of
nonlinear mechanics of thin-walled structures in view of
associated finite element formulations. This means that we
will primarily focus on formulations for structural models
as prerequisite for derivation of finite elements, rather than
specific “elementology”. The main emphasis is put on shells,
including the special case of flat plates, turning into shells
anyway in the nonlinear regime. The derivations are kept
as general as possible, including thick and layered shells
(laminated or sandwich structures), as well as anisotropic
and inhomogeneous materials from the outset. Throughout
Section 5, we will specify the corresponding restrictions
and assumptions to obtain the classical 5-parameter shell
formulation predominantly used for standard shell problems
in the context of finite element analysis.
In most part of this chapter, we restrict ourselves to
static problems within the framework of geometrically
nonlinear elasticity, neglecting time dependence and inertia
effects. The extension into the materially nonlinear area is
a straightforward procedure. It is believed that this does
not mean a too strong restriction in view of the underlying
motivation.
It is a well-cultivated tradition to let review articles start
with the remark that a complete overview of existing methods
and appropriate literature is utterly impossible. The multitude of existing concepts, methods, and implementations, as
well as scientific papers, text books, and yet other review
articles, would effectively prohibit an exhaustive overview.
We therefore directly head toward the ideas and inspirations driving the authors during the compilation of this
chapter.
Motivations for concerning oneself with the present subject
are many. They might be of purely scientific nature or driven
by the need to find the necessary level for the mechanical

3

content of a model or to have a reliable element as a tool
for certain applications one might have in mind. Interest in
the mathematical formulation and resulting numerical properties of finite elements for thin-walled structures may also
arise when simply applying certain finite element formulations available in scientific or commercial codes. While
trying to provide useful information for practitioners and
users of commercial finite element codes, this treatise clearly
addresses a readership with a scientific background, both
things not being mutually exclusive anyway.
When developing and applying a finite element formulation for thin-walled structures, one comes across a couple
of questions. Which mechanical effects should be included
and which can be neglected? Is it better to start from a shell
theory or develop continuum-based elements along the lines
of the degenerated solid approach? And what about geometrically exact models? Which simplifications are useful –
and admissible? Which consequences does the formulation
have for the finite element model? Which parametrization
of degrees of freedom is sensible for the applications one
has in mind? Should drilling degrees of freedom be included
in a shell formulation or not? There are many, many more
questions.
It is in this spirit that we try to give an overview of the
various decisions and assumptions that one implicitly makes
when choosing a specific theory or finite element formulation, respectively. Although such an overview is necessarily incomplete, it should not cover only a mere register
of umpteen different plate and shell finite elements along
with their alleged pros, cons, and limitations; to be even
more specific, this aspect will be very limited in the present
contribution.
By doing this, we carefully separate modeling assumptions
and the finite element formulation. The former addresses
those approximations that are made while formulating
the continuous theory. The latter is concerned with additional approximation errors, coming into play along with
discretization and finite element formulation. While these
numerical errors eventually vanish with mesh refinement,
the model errors, inherent to the theory, persist. Distinguishing this is crucial for a sophisticated determination
of feasible methods and a competent interpretation of
numerical results.

1.3

Notation and conventions

Throughout this chapter, we use both index notation and
absolute notation for the expressions of vectors and tensors,
trying to comply with standard conventions in modern papers
and text books. In general, operators are printed as standard
characters, scalars as italics, and vectors and tensors as bold

not available. This is the case, for instance, in the context

one of the major motivations for the further development of
differential geometry of manifolds.
However, as long as we are dealing with processes taking
place in ℝ3 , with space and time being decoupled, there does
not seem to be a need to plunge into the advanced mathematical framework of differential geometry of manifolds,
other than elegance or generality. Therefore, in the spirit of an
Encyclopedia, we tried to put together concepts and formulae
that are directly accessible to comparison and implementation, at the same time trying to keep compromises with
respect to mathematical rigor within bearable limits.
The position vector of a given point on a surface in
three-dimensional space is denoted by R(𝜉 1 , 𝜉 2 ), where
convective coordinates 𝜉 𝛼 ought to represent a singularityfree parametrization, that is, 𝜉 1 and 𝜉 2 uniquely identify a
specific location on the surface and each location on the
surface is uniquely associated with a pair of coordinates
{𝜉 1 , 𝜉 2 } (Figure 1). We will later on additionally introduce
the 𝜉 3 -direction, indicating the thickness direction needed to
associate the surface with a three-dimensional body.

Continuum mechanics

The mathematical theory of nonlinear continuum mechanics
is treated in numerous papers and text books and there is
neither space nor need at this point to review it in detail.
The intention of this section is to introduce the notation
used in the subsequent sections and to provide some of
the basic formulae needed for the derivations therein. At
the same time, we narrow down the vast field of nonlinear
continuum mechanics to the type of problems that we utilize
in the sequel to develop plate and shell models in view of
their respective finite element discretizations. The mechanical derivations in this section are concluded when arriving
at the field equations and boundary conditions that specify
the boundary-value problem of three-dimensional, nonlinear
elasticity.
As the equilibrium equations and the force boundary conditions are usually represented in the weak form in the context
of finite element methods, a brief description of the underlying variational formulation concludes this section.
Although some remarks on nonlinear dynamics are scattered throughout the chapter, most of the mathematical
derivations are restricted to time-independent problems,
neglecting inertia effects. We will therefore refrain from
discussing topics such as Hamiltonian mechanics. The scalar
variable t will be used as a pseudo-time in order to be able
to parametrize sequences of configurations (i.e., quasi-static
deformations). Confusion with plate or shell thickness, for
which the same variable is used, should be excluded in the
respective context.
For further studies in nonlinear continuum mechanics, we
recommend (in alphabetical order) the treatises by Duvaut
and Lions (1972), Green and Zerna (1968), Gurtin (1981),
Malvern (1969), Marsden and Hughes (1983), Stein and
Barthold (1996), Sokolnikoff (1956), and Truesdell and Noll
(1965).
Point of departure is the introduction of a set 𝔅 of
connected material points 𝔐i , which we identify with a

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

6

body. The surface or boundary of the body is denoted as 𝜕𝔅.
Unless otherwise specified, all equations given in this section
are assumed to be valid in the domain 𝔅. The placement of
𝔅, that is, of its individual material points in space, is called
configuration and can formally be defined as
𝜒 ∶ (𝔅, t) → 𝔖

(25)

The map 𝜒 associates a unique position vector x ∈ ℝ3 in
three-dimensional space with each material point 𝔐i ∈ 𝔅
and time t ∈ [t0 , ∞].
Equation (25) effectively introduces the notion of nonpolar
continuum mechanics. This means that the configuration
at a certain time t is uniquely defined by a set of position vectors x ∈ ℝ3 . Polar continuum mechanical concepts,
usually associated with the names of the Cosserats (Cosserat
and Cosserat, 1909), require additional information about the
orientation of the material points, or the so-called directors
associated with those. This concept will be of some significance in the context of geometrically exact shell formulations, introduced in Section 4.3.
The configuration at t = t0 is called reference configuration, and it is usually understood to be the undeformed
state of the structure. As said already, following a standard convention in continuum mechanics, we mark quantities referring to the reference configuration with uppercase
letters, such that, for instance, X denotes the position vector
to a material point in its placement at time t = t0 . The components X i of X = X i ei are therefore also called material coordinates of the body.
The current configuration of the body at a given time
t > t0 can now be formulated as a map from the reference
configuration onto ℝ3 ,
𝚽 ∶ (X, t) → ℝ3

x = 𝚽(X, t)

for the denomination of the domain of the problem, that is,
the body 𝔅 and its boundary 𝜕𝔅, respectively, because this
notation complies with what one commonly finds in the finite
element-related literature.

The deformation gradient F is a generally nonsymmetric
second-order tensor. In order to uniquely accomplish the
transformations in equations (31), it has to be invertible.
This requires its determinant, the Jacobian J = Det(F) to be
nonzero. Moreover, the map associated with F ought to be
continuous and hence we arrive at the requirement of positive
semidefiniteness
J = Det(F) ≥ 0
(35)
Mechanically, this condition precludes self-penetration of
material.

In order to keep the derivations in the subsequent

Venant–Kirchhoff-type material law. Its application is
sensible for material and structural behavior exhibiting
small strains but large rotations. The framework developed
so far, however, is also directly accessible to materials
leading to large strains like in hyperelasticity or finite
strain elastoplasticity (see, for instance, the text books by
Belytschko et al., 2014 and Simo and Hughes, 1998). The
same is true for formulations for thin-walled structures that
follow.

for the surface traction. Here, û denotes prescribed displacements on the corresponding part Γu of the boundary and ̂t are
prescribed surface tractions on Γ𝜎 .
For a formulation using displacements as primal
unknowns, equation (50) describes Dirichlet boundary conditions, whereas equation (51) the Neumann
boundary conditions. Mixed, so-called Robin boundary
conditions – appearing, for instance, in the case of elastic
support – are not further discussed.
The variational basis for finite element formulations in
structural mechanics are usually energy functionals. For the
simplest and most popular type, namely, displacement-based
finite elements relying on a standard Galerkin formulation
with the displacement field as the only primary unknown, this
is the virtual work principle (sometimes also called principle
of virtual displacements),
𝛿Π =

∫Ω

[S ∶ 𝛿E − 𝜌b ⋅ 𝛿u] dΩ −

∫Γ𝜎

̂t ⋅ 𝛿u dΓ = 0

(52)

Mathematically, equation (52) is the weak form of equilibrium, equation (49), and force boundary conditions, equation
(51). It is subject to two local subsidiary conditions
E = 12 (FT ⋅ F − G)

and

S = S(E)

(53)

representing the kinematic equation and the material law,
and one subsidiary condition on the boundary, namely, the
displacement boundary condition, equation (50). The variation of E can be expressed as
𝛿E = 12 (𝛿FT ⋅ F + FT ⋅ 𝛿F)
= 12 (𝛿u,X + 𝛿uT,X + 𝛿uT,X ⋅ u,X + uT,X ⋅ 𝛿u,X )

(54)

The virtual work principle states that the work done by the
external forces 𝜌b (body forces) and ̂t (surface tractions) in an
arbitrary virtual displacement 𝛿u (also called test function) is
zero if the mechanical system is in a state of static equilibrium. The virtual displacements have to comply with kinematic constraints, that is, they have to be admissible, exactly
complying with the kinematic equations and homogeneous
displacement boundary conditions. The consequence for a
finite element formulation is that shape functions used for
discretization of virtual displacements (test functions) have
to vanish on Γu and those used for displacements (trial functions) have to comply with the displacement boundary conditions (50). Moreover, both test and trial functions ought to
obey certain compatibility and completeness conditions in
order to ensure consistency and thus convergence of the
finite element method. These issues are discussed in detail
in Finite Element Methods.

In the context of functional analysis and variational
calculus, the virtual displacements are called variations. In
the case of conservative problems for which there exists a
potential, equation (52) can be obtained from the principle
of minimum potential energy with the standard methods of
variational calculus. However, in a more general context, it
can also be computed directly from the governing differential
equations, namely, the equilibrium equation and the force
boundary condition, with the help of the Galerkin’s method,
a special instance of the method of weighted residuals. Its
validity does therefore not require existence of a potential.
The corresponding finite elements can be applied directly to
a wide range of nonlinear problems, not presupposing any
external or internal potential.
When applying multifield variational principles, variations of strains and stresses may also appear. This applies,
for instance, to the Hellinger–Reissner principle and the
Hu–Washizu principle, prompting formulation of mixed or
hybrid-mixed finite elements. In those elements, not only
displacements but also stresses and/or strains appear as free
variables.
For treatises on functional analysis and variational principles in mechanics, especially in the context of finite elements,
we recommend (in alphabetical order) Bufler (1983), Felippa
(1989, 1994), Oden and Reddy (1976), Reddy (1998), and
Washizu (1968).

3

PLATES AND SHELLS

Shells are commonly identified as surface-like structures
with one dimension being significantly smaller then the other
two. Geometry of a – sufficiently smooth – shell can be
uniquely identified by defining its mid-surface and a thickness function. Shells play an important role in a lot of fields
of nature and technology. They appear as containers, pipes,
cooling towers, masonry cupolas, airplane fuselages, car
bodies, egg shells, external skeletons of insects, and in an
infinite number of other shapes. Plate and shell structures
are often supplemented by slender stiffeners. They allow
the thin-walled structure to keep their optimal load-carrying
capacity without giving up their lightweight property. A thin
structure with a heterogeneous layout across the thickness,
for example, having a porous lightweight core, aims in the
same direction.
If properly designed as load-carrying structures, shells
exhibit an optimal ratio of stiffness to weight. In this
case, they carry the load exclusively via membrane forces,
that is, forces parallel to the mid-surface. In contrast to
arches, in the case of doubly curved shells, this can be
achieved for more than just one specific load distribution.
On the other hand, especially thin shells can be extremely

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

10

sensitive to imperfections, specifically with regard to
their stability behavior. This mixture of performance and
sensitivity makes the shell the “primadonna of structures”
(Ramm, 1986). It is not at least for this reason that reliable methods for the analysis of shells are particularly
important.
Structural behavior of shells is characterized by two principally different states of deformation, namely, membrane and
bending action. The membrane and bending deformations
of a cylinder investigated in Section 5.9 are typical illustrative examples: the bending-dominated deformation shown
in Figure 7 exhibits typical ovalizing shapes, preserving
lengths of fibers in the mid-surface. In contrast to that,
membrane-dominated deformations, as the radial extension
in Figure 6, come along with strains in the mid-surface, in
this example, due to stretching of material fibers in hoop
direction.
For designers, the membrane state is the desired one
because it optimally makes use of the material, exhibiting
at least approximately a constant stress distribution across
the thickness. In the presence of bending, only the material
fibers away from the mid-surface are used. More importantly, because shells are typically thin, the internal lever
arm is extremely small, such that large internal strains and
stresses are needed to equilibrate external loads via bending
moments. Consequently, shells are much more flexible with
respect to bending than with respect to membrane action.
Pure bending deformations of shells are also called inextensional deformations because there exists a surface within the
shell that is completely free from longitudinal extension (or
compression). Strictly speaking, pure inextensional deformations are only possible for developable structures (flat plates,
cylinders, and cones). However, nearly inextensional deformations have a great practical significance also for doubly
curved shells.
A particular stress situation can be either membrane dominated or bending dominated, depending on the geometry
of the shell, loads, and boundary conditions. Identification of one or the other situation may be accomplished on
the basis of the asymptotic behavior of the shell as thickness approaches zero. The ratio of membrane energy to
bending energy then either approaches infinity or zero, indicating a membrane-dominated situation in the former case
and a bending-dominated one in the latter. Practical situations usually involve both membrane and bending action.
However, for a well-designed load-carrying shell, structural
behavior should be dominated by membrane action and
bending may be present mainly due to unavoidable circumstances such as concentrated forces or incompatibilities near
boundaries. In the latter case, bending moments appear in the
shape of boundary layers, the dimensions of which depend on
shell thickness.

11

On the other hand, the feature of inextensible deformations may also be exploited in special cases. For
example, deployable and inflatable structures should be
designed to benefit from these bending-dominated transition processes. Antennae unfolding in space, tape-like
structures, for example, videotapes, and pneumatic structures under internal prestress are typical examples. They
benefit from bending during the folding phase, eventually
leading to a membrane-dominated situation for their final
application.
To illustrate some typical phenomena, we investigate
the simple case of a clamped hemispherical shell subject
to dead load in a numerical experiment. In Figure 3, the
normalized energy fractions emanating from membrane
forces and bending moments are plotted versus shell
slenderness. The shell essentially carries the load by
membrane action but exhibits the typical boundary layer at
the clamped edge. It results from the geometric constraint
imposed by the boundary conditions, which is contradictory to a pure membrane state. The colored plots in
Figure 3 show the distribution of the corresponding energy
fractions. Clearly, the diminishing edge effect can be
observed, reducing bending energy eventually to a tiny
boundary layer effect. As a consequence, bending and
membrane energy interchange their dominance from thick
to thin.
The gradual transition from an initially bending-dominated
structure into a membrane dominated structure is also often
the natural result of a nonlinear large deformation process
during loading. A typical example is a well-supported flat
plate being loaded perpendicular to its surface, exploiting the
membrane or hammock effect as deflections get large and
the flat plate transforms into a curved shell. In other words,
the structure deforms into a shape, allowing for an optimal
load-carrying behavior; it escapes from bending.
In mathematical literature, the terms inhibited and noninhibited inextensional deformations are sometimes used,
meaning that pure bending deformations are either excluded,
for instance, by geometry or boundary conditions, or not
excluded (Bathe and Chapelle, 2003). Those terms, however,
are not equivalent to the notions “membrane dominated”
and “bending dominated”.
Owing to the high flexibility of thin shells with respect to
bending, this state of deformation has the strongest impact
on nonlinear behavior of shells. But also shells in a pure
membrane state may exhibit strong nonlinearities in the form
of a sudden transition to a bending-dominated state. This
phenomenon, for example, through buckling, is typical for
thin-walled structures and represents the reason for many
spectacular accidents (Ramm and Wall, 2004), Buckling.
The issue of instability and buckling is treated in some detail
in Section 6.

See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

4

DIMENSIONAL REDUCTION AND
STRUCTURAL MODELS

4.1

Basic concept

In nonpolar continuum theory, not focusing on specific
structures, or contact and interaction problems, mechanical modeling almost exclusively concerns the constitutive
law. Specific choices of strain measures, time integration algorithms, finite element formulations, or spatial
discretization may have considerable impact on numerical
behavior and approximation quality. However, the exact
solution of a continuous initial boundary-value problem
only differs if a different constitutive law is applied. This
does not only include solid mechanics but also general
continua, for instance, fluids, for which the kinematic
equations, equilibrium, balance of mass, and momentumare
identical.
The notion of a structural model comes into play as soon
as information about geometry or fabrication properties of
a certain class of structures are used for an a priori modification of the three-dimensional equations. In the context
of thin-walled structures such as plates and shells, these
modifications consist in dimensional reduction from three to
two dimensions. Different techniques to obtain such models

are outlined in Sections 4.2–4.4. The respective modeling
assumptions are addressed in Section 5.
Historically, structural models have been developed to
simplify the governing differential equations in order to make
a certain class of problems accessible to analytical solution
methods, impossible to apply in the three-dimensional case
due to the complexity of the equations, mostly because
of the boundary conditions. In many cases, such models
are inspired by a sophisticated insight into the structural behavior, rather than being obtained from a formal
analytical dimensional reduction. The ingenious assumption, commonly attributed to Leonhard Euler and Jakob
Bernoulli, of initially straight cross sections remaining
straight throughout deformation is a classical example for
this. Often, such reduced models have been established ab
initio without making use of (or even having available) the
underlying three-dimensional equations.
These models are indispensable in all fields of structural
engineering and consequently, related computational strategies have been developed during the advent of finite element
methods. While there is no need to simplify differential
equations when a weak form is used to construct approximate solutions, application of structural models instead of
the complete three-dimensional theory in the context of
finite element analysis still includes the advantage of saving

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

12

degrees of freedom and thus computing time. A second,
not less important, advantage of the dimensional reduction
is condensation of the three-dimensional response and the
related mechanical insight into typical “engineering phenomena”, such as stretching, bending, transverse shear deformation, or twisting.
Given the ongoing developments in the computer industry
and the seemingly unlimited progress in computational
power, one might argue that these advantages are becoming
more and more obsolete. There is, however, much more to
the models developed by our ancestors than merely reducing
the number of unknowns in an abstract mathematical theory.
Structural models effectively reproduce the substantial
aspects of real mechanical behavior, observed in experiments, while omitting subsidiary effects. As an example,
take the stress distribution of a clamped plate under uniform
load. The exact solution according to three-dimensional
theory of elasticity yields singularities in the stress distribution near the edges and one has to take into account nonlinear
constitutive models, such as elastoplasticity or damage, in
order to obtain realistic results. A simple, linear-elastic
Kirchhoff plate theory, however, renders approximate results
that match experimental evidence quite well even when
a simple linear material model is used. We may conclude
that structural models are more than just simplifications
of a three-dimensional theory. Nevertheless, it is sensible
to take the three-dimensional equations as a reference for
quantification of the influence of certain simplifications,
which is what we will do in Section 5.
Historically, the first plate and shell models have been
developed heuristically, for instance, on the basis of Euler’s
and Bernoulli’s assumptions mentioned above. Later, these
models have been provided with a mathematical basis by
comparison with the exact three-dimensional equations
by means of asymptotic analysis Morgenstern (1959),
Friedrichs and Dressler (1961), Gol’denveizer and Kolos
(1965), Dauge and Gruais (1996), Arnold and Falk (1996),
Paumier and Raoult (1997), and Ciarlet (1990) and Plates
and Shells: Asymptotic Expansions and Hierarchical
Models for an overview). A plate or shell model is said to
be asymptotically correct if the model solution converges
toward the three-dimensional solution in the limit of
vanishing thickness. It was, for instance, not before one
century later that Kirchhoff’s plate theory from Kirchhoff
(1850) has been justified mathematically by Morgenstern
(1959), who proved its asymptotic correctness.
It is important to remark that the error in the thin limit has
to be estimated by specifically designed measures in order to
obtain convergence. In general situations, the model solution
will not reproduce the three-dimensional one exactly at each
location in space (a simple example are the aforementioned
singularities, which do not vanish in the thin limit).

13

It turns out that a certain balance between
through-the-thickness distribution of strain and stress is
crucial for the resulting model to be asymptotically correct
(Libai and Simmonds, 1998), necessitating a modification
of the material law for lower order models.
Developing plate and shell models essentially constitutes
a spatial semidiscretization of the continuous problem.
There is an immense number of different models, a mere
enumeration of which does not make sense. We will therefore focus on description of principle strategies to realize
dimensional reduction (Sections 4.2–4.4) as well as the
related assumptions (Section 5). It will turn out that apparently different philosophies, namely deriving shell theories
from three-dimensional continuum mechanics, Section 4.2,
directly establishing two-dimensional equations, Section
4.3, or developing finite shell elements by “degenerating”
solid elements, Section 4.4, are in fact identical if the same
model assumptions are made.
Conceptually, however, derivation from the threedimensional continuum equations is probably the most
general approach because it naturally includes arbitrarily
complex models, including hierarchic higher order models
or layer-wise theories, useful for computation of composites
and laminates.
Throughout Section 5 we will specialize the mentioned
modeling assumptions and discretization to a standard conventional 5-parameter shell model with
Reissner–Mindlin kinematics. This means that five
independent variables, namely, three mid-surface
displacements and two independent rotations, are used
for parametrization of displacements. Thus, as rotations
are independent of the gradients of the mid-surface, this
model allows for transverse shear deformations, a feature
that is commonly associated with the names of Reissner
(1945) and Mindlin (1951). Like this paragraph, the
corresponding parts of this chapter are set within a frame
for easy identification.

4.2

Derivation from three-dimensional continuum

A natural way to look at models or theories for thin-walled
structures is their interpretation as a certain approximation
of the three-dimensional equations. We will refer to the
three-dimensional theory of elasticity in most parts of this
chapter. This, however, does not mean that the described
concepts are restricted to elasticity problems. They are
equally well suited to treat problems involving plasticity,
damage, or other kinds of material nonlinearities.
The modus operandi to obtain a reduced model from
the three-dimensional equations unfolding in what follows

focuses on shells, but it is applicable to beams and plates
as well. While plates can be viewed as a special case of
shells, the modifications to obtain general beam formulations
are more complex, since dimensional reduction reduces the
three-dimensional continuum to a one-dimensional structure.
Here, in particular, torsion introduces some complexity.
The original development of the direct approach is mostly
attributed to Ericksen and Truesdell (1958). An excellent
and exhaustive treatise on the subject has been given by
Naghdi (1972). Without exactly reproducing the details of
his derivations, the principal concept is described in the
following.

The approximation of the displacement field u(𝜉 i ) established in equation (60) can be interpreted as a semidiscretization of the three-dimensional continuum in one spatial
dimension, namely, the transverse direction 𝜉 3 . The corresponding shape functions are the monomials (𝜉 3 )N and the
parameters (degrees of freedom) vN are functions of the
in-plane coordinates 𝜉 1 , 𝜉 2 . In order to accomplish a certain
balance between in-plane and transverse strain distribution,
the displacement expansion (60) may be supplemented by
additional conditions, like inextensibility of the director
R1 = D (cf. equation (17)), in the case of Kirchhoff–Love
and Reissner-Mindlin-type models.
It may also be sensible to choose different orders of approximation for in-plane and transverse displacements. In order to
avoid an awkward notation, we are not attempting to cast all
possible parametrizations of the through-the-thickness distribution of the displacements into a general formal framework.
Specific models of this kind are put into more concrete terms
in Section 5.
The unknowns represent displacements, or generalized
displacements, such as rotations and cross-sectional warping,
in the abovementioned semidiscretization on the basis of
the virtual work principle. Similar to finite element formulations, alternative multifield variational approaches could
be utilized as well. For instance, the shell models proposed
by Büchter and Ramm (1992a) (see also Büchter et al.,
1994; Betsch et al., 1995; Bischoff and Ramm, 1997, among
many others), based on the enhanced assumed strain (EAS)
method (Simo and Rifai, 1990) following a modified version
of the Hu–Washizu principle, can be assigned to this group.

The monomials used as shape functions in equation (60)
represent just one out of numerous possibilities. The shape
functions neither have to be monomials nor do they need
to satisfy more than C0 -continuity, that is, for instance, a
layer-wise linear approach is possible as well. The most
important models are described later in Section 5.11.
Computation of strains and stresses from the displacements
is straightforward in the sense that the standard equations
of the three-dimensional theory are applied. For first-order
models with linear approximation of transverse displacements, additional measures have to be taken in order to obtain
an asymptotically correct theory. The commonly applied
strategy is to modify the material equations by explicitly
setting transverse normal stress to zero (Section 5.7). Note
that this does not establish a plane stress assumption, because
transverse shear stresses are still nonzero, even for the
simplest shell models.

strains and stresses as well as the internal and external
energies are exact as well. Approximations come into play
as soon as the sums describing geometry, load, and displacements are restricted to a finite number of components,
neglecting higher order terms. Further assumptions affect
through-the-thickness distribution of strains and stresses,
the material law, and its preintegration and certain approximations of the shifter Z. The bottom line of this concept is
that the deformation of the three-dimensional shell body is
approximated by carefully chosen functions of the thickness
coordinate 𝜉 3 .

4.3

Direct approach

The principal idea of the direct approach can be traced back
to the theory of the brothers (Cosserat and Cosserat, 1909)
who proposed an extension of the classical continuum, introducing rotations of a material point as additional degrees
of freedom. The concept, also known as micropolar theory,
has been picked up in the 1950/1960s for different applications and was also utilized in an elegant way to model rotations of the cross-sectional fibers of thin-walled structures.
Thus, a plate or shell may be represented as a “directed”
two-dimensional surface. For an extensive discussion confer
(Rubin, 2000).
Shell models of this group are also often denoted as
geometrically exact (Simo and Fox, 1989), but this does not
mean that the approximation of the actual three-dimensional
geometry of the deformed shell structure is necessarily more
accurate than in other models. It merely reflects the fact
that the concept is not derived from geometric approximations of a three-dimensional body but starts from an
exact kinematic description of a two-dimensional Cosserat
surface. Moreover, it does not introduce any restrictions with
respect to the size of rotations (cf. Section 5.10). The crucial
model assumptions are thus concentrated in the constitutive equations, that is, three-dimensional theory of elasticity
comes into play as soon as the material law is defined.
Apparently, there is no unique definition of what a “geometrically exact” model would be. In this chapter, we
follow the concept introduced by Juan Simo and coworkers,
where the geometrically exact approach is confronted
with the so-called degenerated solid approach, treated
in the following section. Accordingly, the only difference is in the way reduction to resultant form, and thus
through-the-thickness integration, is accomplished (analytical through-the-thickness integration in the former and
numerical quadrature in the latter). In this sense, the geometrically exact approach or direct approach in the context of
finite element analysis may well be understood as being
based on classical shell theory.

In contrast to the previous section, in which shell
equations are obtained by a systematic dimensional reduction neglecting higher order terms, in the present framework,
the governing equations are merely postulated. Of course,
this is done with the intention to comply with mechanically
sensible assumptions in order to obtain an asymptotically
correct theory.
As in the previous section, the shell theory unfolds on
the basis of kinematic (geometric) considerations. From
a general perspective, this concept describes a directed
continuum in which the placement of a material point is specified not only by its position vector but also by a set of directors associated with each material point. In the present case,
an inextensible one-director Cosserat surface is utilized.
Mathematically, the geometric and kinematic description
of the shell represents a differentiable manifold
 = {(r, d) ∶  ⊂ ℝ2 → ℝ3 ×  2 }

(91)

where  2 is defined as the unit sphere
 2 = {d ∈ ℝ3 ∶ ||d|| = 1}

(92)

||∘|| being the Euclidean norm in ℝ3 (cf. equation (5)).
Unlike the three-dimensional configuration space used in
continuum mechanics,  is not a linear space, because its
representation contains the nonlinear manifold  2 . We will
see in the following that this introduces certain peculiarities
in the parametrization of the deformation, in particular, the
rotations needed for the director update.
The parameter space  ⊂ ℝ2 is an open set, representing
the mid-surface of the shell. We shall assume for the subsequent derivations that it has a sufficiently smooth boundary
𝜕. The closure of  is referred to as ̄ =  ∪ 𝜕A. ℝ2
and ℝ3 represent the usual real-valued two and three spaces,
respectively.
The essence of equation (91) is that r ∶  → ℝ3 maps
points of the shell’s parameter space into three-dimensional
space, thus describing the deformation of its mid-surface

The kinematic assumptions associated with the map
Φ ∶ 𝔖0 → 𝔖 are identical to that of a shear-deformable
first-order theory, assuming initially straight cross-sectional
fibers remaining straight and unstretched throughout deformation, allowing for transverse shear strains and neglecting
thickness change. Moreover, initial shell thickness t is
assumed to be constant with respect to 𝜉 𝛼 . A similar model
can be obtained from the concept described in the previous
section by appropriate assumptions for displacements and
geometry.
The direct approach is not restricted to this model but
rather general. Given appropriate definitions of , the same
multitude of models as described in the previous section
can be derived (see, for instance, Simo et al., 1989 for a
formulation including through-the-thickness stretching).
A crucial point in the development of geometrically exact
shell formulations is parametrization of the rotations. We
dedicate a good part of Section 7.3 to this topic and therefore
postpone provision of the underlying mathematical definitions to this section. The remarks therein are also valid in
the context of continuum-based shell elements. For the time
being, we take for granted that a unique parametrization of a
rotation tensor 𝚲 ∈ SD2 ⊂ SO(3) is at hand (assuming smooth
shells). The corresponding rotation vector 𝜽 is uniquely specified by two independent parameters and maps the director
from the undeformed (reference) configuration onto the
deformed (current) configuration,
d =𝚲⋅D

(95)

without introducing drilling around the director D. Here, 𝚲
is a rotation tensor depending on 𝜽. Moreover, we assume
in the following that D = A3 (cf. equation (17)), that is, the
director field is orthogonal to the mid-surface of the shell.
For smooth shells, this can be done without loss of generality
for the resulting shell theory. In the case of a discretization method, however, the question of orthogonality of the
director field reenters the focus of interest. As this is a question of discretization rather than theory, this is discussed in
more detail in Section 7.2.

Equations (115–117) differ in a couple of aspects from
the format that emanates from a derivation from the
three-dimensional continuum, equations (83) and (80). First,
the above equations are obtained from the assumption of

Section 5.7, below, for details). Second, definition of the
components H ijkl of the constitutive tensor relies on the

directly from (80) by replacing Gij by Aij in the formulae for
Cijkl therein and introducing the aforementioned condition
S33 = 0. As Aij is constant in 𝜉 3 , through-the-thickness
integration is accomplished analytically, independent of
shell geometry. This means at the same time that information about initial curvature of the shell is neglected in the
components of the material tensor in the present approach.
Technically, this assumption is identical to assuming Z = G
in the constitutive law, which is discussed in detail in Section
5.6.

4.4

N K = 14 (1 + 𝜉 K 𝜉)(1 + 𝜂 K 𝜂)

In contrast to what has been described in Sections 4.2 and
4.3, the concept of degeneration is directly related to the
formulation of finite elements, that is, there is no such thing
as a shell theory based on this concept. Nevertheless, strong
similarities to finite elements based on shell theories exist.
In fact, the final finite element result may even be identical
although the path of derivation is different, provided the same
mechanical and numerical assumptions are chosen.
In rough terms, the idea of developing shell finite elements
via degeneration means switching the sequence of dimensional reduction and discretization. Thus, starting point
is a finite element discretization of the three-dimensional
continuum. Shell elements based on the degenerated solid
approach are therefore also denoted as continuum-based
shell elements. Linear shape functions in thickness direction
are used, thus naturally implementing the assumption of
cross-sectional material fibers remaining straight as is typical
for shell theories with Kirchhoff–Love or Reissner–Mindlin
kinematics. However, in principle also higher order functions or more than one element in thickness direction are
possible (Section 7.1.4).
Following an isoparametric concept, identical shape functions are used for discretization of geometry and displacements within an individual element,

vectors to bottom and top nodes, as illustrated in Figure 4.

Further, N K (𝜉, 𝜂) are two-dimensional shape functions, with
𝜉 and 𝜂 representing the in-plane coordinates of the shell
element; 𝜁 denotes the normalized thickness coordinate. As
is customary in finite elements, their range is chosen to be
−1 ≤ {𝜉, 𝜂, 𝜁} ≤ 1 within on element.

Normalization of DK and dK via the nodal thicknesses tK =
||XKtop − XKbot || is not necessary, but is used here to facilitate
comparison to finite elements derived from shell theories.
Note that equations (131) and (132) establish interpolation of
nodal vectors of length tK ||DK || = tK instead of interpolating
thickness t and normalized directors D separately, which is
not equivalent.

based on quantities defined on the mid-surface of the shell,
is thus given as

Here, the vector vK = rK − RK represents the displacement
of a mid-surface node and the so-called difference vector
wK = dK − DK updates the director vector.
It is noticeable that with the given parametrization, six
independent variables are used to describe the deformation at each node, namely, three independent components
each for vK and wK . In comparison to the 5-parameter
models described in the previous section, assuming an
inextensible director, this 6-parameter formulation naturally
includes a thickness change of the shell. This is obviously
a result of deriving the parametrization directly from a
three-dimensional solid element. In fact, the kinematical

content of this formulation is of course identical to that of
a solid element with nodes on either surface with a linear
displacement field across the thickness. Although this additional feature may seem attractive in the first place, because
it potentially enhances the capabilities of the resulting
shell elements in the range of thick shells, there are two
major reasons why this simple formulation needs additional
modifications.
Firstly, and most importantly, the resulting shell element
is not asymptotically correct. The reason is, that bending
cannot be represented properly due to the presence of
artificial normal stresses in thickness direction. This
phenomenon, called thickness locking or Poisson thickness locking in the literature, is explained in more detail in
Section 5.7. Secondly, conditioning of the corresponding
element stiffness matrices is much worse than in conventional 5-parameter shell elements due to the extremely high
stiffness in transverse normal direction (Simo et al., 1989;
Wall et al., 2000).
Possibilities to preserve through-the-thickness stretching
within the shell model, thus formulating three-dimensional
shell elements, are outlined in Section 5. In the remainder
of this chapter, however, we follow the classical concept
of Ahmad et al. (1968) (see also Ahmad et al., 1970)
and for arbitrarily large rotations, Ramm (1976, 1977),
to derive a 5-parameter shell element on the basis of the
fundamental postulate that transverse normal stresses are
negligible. Transverse normal strains E33 (i.e., the thickness
stretch) are thus no longer independent of the in-plane strains

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

22

but result from those due to Poisson’s effect. The degree
of freedom describing the thickness change thus becomes
dispensable, leading to the use of an inextensible director.
This modification implements the additional condition

for the difference vector wK . It can be realized quite naturally

previous section (see also Section 7.3) at node K. To sum

The aforementioned zero transverse normal stress assumption is usually accomplished by modifying the material
equations, eliminating E33 via static condensation. It is worth
a remark that this procedure is not an exclusive quality of
the degenerated solid approach but also appears within classical plate and shell theory (see, for instance, Timoshenko
and Woinowsky-Krieger, 1959). An easy implementation
that is valid for arbitrary anisotropic materials in the range
of small elastic strains is elaborated in Section 5.7. For the
time being, we only use the result in the form of a modified three-dimensional St. Venant-Kirchhoff-type material
law for the isotropic case (cf. equation (47))

23

transverse normal stress condition is effectively included
in the computation of the internal energy (equation (158))
because the energetically conjugate strain component is identically zero. The often used notion “plane-stress” condition
is strictly speaking not correct since transverse shear stresses
are present as well, although they do not show up in the
energy if a Kirchhoff-Love-type model is used.
In a practical finite element implementation, it is customary
to use Voigt notation, placing strain and stress components
in column vectors. Using equation (137) along with a stress
vector that does not take into account transverse normal
stresses effectively implements the necessary conditions to
obtain an asymptotically correct model.

A substantial difference in comparison to equation (105),
obtained in the course of the direct approach, is the fact that
the linear part of the transverse shear strains with respect
to the thickness coordinate 𝜉 3 is in general nonzero. The
reason is the interpolation of nodal directors DK , which does
not preserve the individual director lengths. Note that this is
not an approximation or additional assumption but a direct
result of straightforwardly applying the degenerated solid
approach. In other words, it is a consequence of switching
the sequence of dimensional reduction and discretization.
In view of a finite element formulation, this is neither an
advantage nor a drawback of the degenerated solid approach
compared to finite elements derived from shell theory. If – for
instance, in the context of a finite element formulation based
on a geometrically exact shell model – an interpolation of the
directors is applied without subsequent normalization within
the element domain, the same issue comes up (although it
may not be taken into account explicitly because the corresponding strain components have been removed beforehand
when deriving the governing equations of the underlying
shell theory).
Up to here, discretization and modification of the material
law have been accomplished. The mechanical ingredients necessary for a correct description of the mechanical
behavior of shells, particularly in the thin limit, are thus
provided and it is possible to directly formulate finite
elements on the basis of the given equations. Computation
of the stiffness matrix then involves (numerical) integration

Particularly in the context of the degenerated solid
approach, this has been termed explicit thickness integration or preintegration as opposed to the continuum-typical
implicit thickness integration in which Gauss quadrature is
applied directly to the volume integral. The main advantage
of explicit or preintegration of the material law is that the
number of operations is reduced. In the context of deriving
a shell theory along the lines of Section 4.2 or 4.3, this has
been a natural ingredient of dimensional reduction.
Equations (161–163) exactly correspond to the definition in equation (80) (derivation from three-dimensional
continuum), specialized for a 5-parameter model. Equations
(119–121) (direct approach) can be recovered by assuming
Zh = Gh in the definition of the components C̄ ijkl and Zh = 1
in the evaluation of the integral, along with introducing a
shear correction factor. These similarities again confirm the
strong interrelation between the different approaches.

from which the consistent nodal forces and moments referring to mid-surface displacements and rotation parameters

are no special peculiarities here and the consistent nodal
force vector is obtained in the standard manner in the context
of a finite element formulation.
Finite elements based on the degenerated solid approach
are exactly equivalent to finite elements based on a shell
theory if the same model assumptions, parametrization, and
interpolation of degrees of freedom are used – an issue
that has been discussed for a considerable amount of time
(Büchter, 1992; Büchter and Ramm, 1992b). The crucial
questions concern assumptions for the shifter Z associated
with preintegration of the material law as well as interpolation of the rotation parameters or the director, respectively.

5

MODELING ASSUMPTIONS

5.1

Motivation and overview

In the precomputer era, development of shell theories has
frequently been driven by the desire to obtain equations
that are accessible to analytical or simple numerical solution methods, often restricted to specific geometries. Consequently, many assumptions have established a compromise
between modeling the most important mechanical features
and obtaining manageable equations. The related assumptions have been the source of many academic disputes and

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

Models and Finite Elements for Thin-Walled Structures

Models and Finite Elements for Thin-Walled Structures

from todays point of view they do not always appear to
be systematic. During the sixties, particularly the works of
Koiter and Naghdi provided an important basis for a systematic development of shell equations (see Koiter, 1960 as well
as Naghdi, 1972 for an overview).
In this section, the principal assumptions and modeling
decisions on the way to a certain shell model along with
their respective consequences are discussed from a mechanical perspective. Some of the material is taken from the PhD
thesis of the third author (Irslinger, 2013). We try to carefully
separate these modeling assumptions from those approximations and assumptions made along with a specific finite
element discretization, which are discussed in Section 7. In
the context of a finite element formulation, the statements
made in this section are therefore only valid in the limit
h → 0, h being the typical element size.
Out of the three different possibilities to obtain shell finite
elements outlined in Sections 4.2–4.4, we choose derivation
from the three-dimensional continuum equations, described
in Section 4.2, as a main guideline.

5.2

Criteria for an optimal approximation

Naghdi (1963) was probably the first to describe criteria that
ought to be taken into consideration while developing new
shell theories. The work has been motivated by the observation that results obtained with different theories significantly
differed for a given problem. Moreover, the question naturally arose whether there would be a “best” shell theory. This
term has first been used by Budiansky and Sanders (1963).
Later, Krätzig (1973) (see also Krätzig, 1993) further elaborated on the question of feasible criteria and optimal shell
models. He enhanced and further specified Naghdi’s criteria
as follows:
1.
2.
3.

Invariance toward coordinate transformations.
Invariance toward static rigid-body rotations.
Equal approximation of all governing equations, that is,
(a) conservation of energy,
(b) conservation of mass,
(c) entropy production,
(d) equations of motion and
(e) symmetrie conditions (following the axiom of a
Boltzmann continuum).

The first two conditions are taken from Naghdi (1963).
The remaining conditions essentially state that the approximation errors are of equal order in all aspects of continuum
mechanics. Such an equal approximation is desirable,
because the largest error dominates the overall solution
and higher accuracy in only some of the individual criteria
does not improve the situation. In what follows, the focus is

on the approximation of the mechanical equations, that is,
criterion 3(d).

5.3

Topological and material setup of the shell
structure

Before addressing the typical restrictions of shell models
regarding description of deformations, model assumptions
for the undeformed state have to be discussed. This concerns,
in particular, initial shell geometry and material setup. In
principle, many three-dimensional bodies can be described
as assemblies of thick shells. It is practical, however, to
restrict our considerations to bodies that topologically allow
for a proper definition of a mid-surface and a thickness direction, that is, a compact body with a simply connected structure in thickness direction, or, in other words, a solid cross
section. The typical shell assumptions such as “small thickness”, “small curvature”, or “shallow shell” are not needed
for the time being. The framework described in Section 4.2
allows for arbitrarily fine models, capable of dealing with
theoretically infinitely thick shells.
In practical applications, the three-dimensional geometry
of the shell body is often generated inversely by first defining
a surface and then assigning a thickness function to it. The
latter is restricted by the condition that the shell thickness has
to be smaller than twice the smallest radius of curvature in
order to avoid self-penetration of material. As an illustrative
example, consider the limiting case of a cylindrical shell
with t = 2R, which is essentially a solid cylinder. One might
argue that violation of this condition within small regions,
for instance, in the case of shells with kinks, is acceptable.
However, the fact that Det(Z) = Z ≤ 0 in these cases may
cause problems. The practical treatment of shells with kinks
in the context of finite element formulations is discussed in
Section 7.2.
In a lot of practical applications both geometric and material properties are inhomogeneous with respect to the thickness direction. This is, for instance, true for shells made
up of composite materials, such as reinforced concrete or
fiber-reinforced polymers. Woven fabrics are not homogeneous materials but have a certain geometric structure that
varies through the thickness. The same is true in lightweight
sandwich structures with a cellular, for example, honeycomb, internal layer, bounded by solid panels at the outer
surfaces, again leading to a nonhomogeneous geometrical
layout.
As far as geometry is concerned, the framework mentioned
in the introduction of Section 5 implies a homogenization
in thickness direction, smearing out geometrical heterogeneities or voids along 𝜉 3 -direction. The most general
geometric description of the shell structure is then given

See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

If we choose fXN = (𝜉 3 )N and let NX → ∞, equation (56)
is recovered, meaning that arbitrarily curved initial
cross-sectional material fibers may be described. In
the context of layer-wise models, used for the analysis of laminated structures, fXN may well represent
C0 -continuous, piecewise linear (or higher order)
functions.
In most cases, however, it is practical to assume straight
initial cross sections, also in the case of laminated structures. The corresponding formula for the position vector
in the reference state has been given in equation (57). In
order to comply with the usual notation, we identify R0
as the position vector R to a point on the mid-surface and
R1 as the director vector D, such that
X(𝜉 1 , 𝜉 2 , 𝜉 3 ) = R(𝜉 1 , 𝜉 2 ) + 𝜉 3 D(𝜉 1 , 𝜉 2 )

(167)

(cf. Sections 4.3 and 4.4).
Technically, representation of an arbitrarily inhomogeneous material is not a problem, provided a feasible analytic
description of the involved material parameters is available.
However, a highly sophisticated representation of material
properties as functions of the thickness coordinate 𝜉 3 obviously only makes sense if, on the other hand, the related
geometry and deformations also reflect this model fidelity.
Accuracy of both geometric and material description have to
retain a certain balance.

5.4

27

Displacements and rotations

One of the fundamental aspects distinguishing different shell
models is the assumption concerning expansion of displacements in 𝜉 3 -direction. Terms such as “Kirchhoff–Love”
or “Koiter” are often used as equivalent to the assumptions of cross sections remaining straight and rotations
depending on the gradient of the mid-surface. The notion
“Reissner–Mindlin” stands for independence of rotations
(i.e., the linear part of the in-plane displacements) from the
mid-surface displacements, thus including the effect of transverse shear strains (theories of this kind are sometimes also
called first-order shear deformation theories, FOSTs).
Having a closer look at the individual scientific contributions reveals subtle differences in models and mathematical
formulations, though. For instance, assumptions concerning
transverse normal stresses are different in Reissner’s plate
theory, Reissner (1944), compared to the one put forward by
Mindlin (1951). Those details, however, play a subsidiary
role in the formulation of finite elements, because they are
overlapped by errors made due to interpolation and numerical integration. In many cases, including the aforementioned
example, they do not even have an impact on the element
stiffness matrix.
Similar to the position vector X in the previous section, the
displacement u is expanded in thickness direction,
u(𝜉 , 𝜉 , 𝜉 ) =

for in-plane and transverse displacements may be employed
(not represented in the formulae used here). A position vector
in the deformed configuration is thus

In a majority of practical applications, it is common to
use a homogenized material description where material
parameters such as Young’s modulus E, Poisson’s ratio
𝜈, or yield stress 𝜎y are constant with respect to the
thickness coordinate. For inhomogenous shells, such as
composites and laminates, homogenized material laws
can be obtained by mixing rules.
If a higher fidelity is needed, a detailed modeling across
the thickness may be realized by layer-wise theories (Section
5.11.4). Another aspect of material setup concerns the representation of shell curvature in the components of the material
tensor C, which is treated separately in Section 5.7.

Following the aforementioned philosophy of semidiscretization in thickness direction on the basis of a standard displacement formulation, the “shape functions” fuN assign the model
accuracy. Setting fuN = (𝜉 3 )N exactly reproduces equation
(60). A better choice than simple monomials are Legendre
polynomials or hierarchical shape functions. Application
of C0 -continuous, piecewise linear (or higher order) functions is recommendable for laminated shells with layer-wise
different material properties. Hereby, the piecewise definition of fuN (𝜉 3 ) is quite naturally suggested by the layered

setup of the shell. A brief discussion of higher order models
of both kinds (i.e., either using polynomials or layer-wise

Setting Nu = NX = 1 along with fuN = (𝜉 3 )N yields
u = v + 𝜉3w

(170)

x = X + u = R + v + 𝜉 3 (D + w)

(171)

such that

As before, we switch to a more convenient notation,
reducing the number of indices, by letting v ∶= v0 and
w ∶= v1 . This also complies with much of the pertinent
literature. The classical displacement assumptions of a
5-parameter, shear-deformable shell model additionally
involve the restriction
||D + w|| = ||D||

(172)

that is, inextensibility of the director. This condition is
effectively established using a rotation tensor 𝚲 instead of
the difference displacement w. The deformation can thus
be described by
x = r + 𝜉3d = R + v + 𝜉3𝚲 ⋅ D

(173)

Feasible parametrizations of this equation are discussed
in detail in Section 7.3.
Equation (171) represents the classical assumption of
cross sections remaining straight throughout deformation.
Transverse shear strain effects are included because no
relationship has been established between rotations and
mid-surface gradients, that is, normals in the reference
configuration need not be normal in the deformed state.
These fundamental assumptions restrict the model
to the effects of in-plane strains and transverse shear
strains. Transverse normal strains are excluded via the
inextensibility condition in equation (172). In order to
avoid artificial transverse normal stresses emanating
from this constraint, and thus to obtain an asymptotically
correct shell theory, a modification of the material law as
described in Section 5.7 is usually employed.
Another popular possibility for parametrization of
displacements within a shell theory is to circumvent the
use of a rotation tensor, sticking to the parametrization with
difference displacements w introduced in equation (170).
The additional inextensibility condition for the director is
set aside, thus allowing for thickness change of the shell

throughout deformation. The resulting 6-parameter model
includes constant transverse normal strains, but lacks a
corresponding linear part, which is why it still requires
a modification of the material law in order to lead to an
asymptotically correct theory for cases in which Poisson’s
ratio is nonzero (Section 5.7).
It may, however, be desirable in certain cases to make use
of unmodified three-dimensional constitutive models, especially when applying materially nonlinear formulations or in
the presence of large strains. Therefore, various strategies of
extensions to a 7-parameter model have been developed.
The seventh parameter may be introduced as an additional
displacement component in transverse direction, leading to
a quadratic distribution of transverse displacements and thus
linearly varying transverse normal strains. The resulting shell
theory is asymptotically correct without any further manipulations of the material law. Three-dimensional shell formulations of this kind have been proposed by Simo et al.
(1989), Verhoeven (1992), Kühhorn and Schoop (1992),
Sansour (1995), and Parisch (1995), among others (see also
Hildebrand et al., 1949 for an early contribution pointing
toward this direction).
We will refer to this concept as a “7-parameter model based
on displacements” in the sequel in order to distinguish it
from the alternative version of a 7-parameter model based
on additional strain parameters, which will be treated in the
following section. For an elegant method to avoid kinematically three-dimensional models while facilitating the use of
three-dimensional material laws, see Klinkel and Govindjee
(2002).
In the formulation of shell, finite elements often drilling
rotations around the director or the shell normal are utilized.
They have been ignored in this section because within the
framework of nonpolar continuum mechanics those rotations
do not exist as independent degrees of freedom. We will
elaborate on this topic later in Section 7.3.

5.5

Strains and kinematic equations

Within a nonlinear theory, application of various alternative
strain measures is possible. We make use of the Green–
Lagrange strain tensor E, defined in equation (36), which is
probably the most popular choice in structural mechanics.
It ought to be noted, though, that the choice of a specific
strain measure in fact influences the resulting shell model in
the geometrically nonlinear regime (Pietraszkiewicz, 1986;
Sansour and Bufler, 1992; Büchter, 1992). In this sense, the
choice of Green–Lagrange strain components represents the
first modeling decision in this section.
If semidiscretization in thickness direction is carried out
on the basis of a standard displacement model with displacements (and possibly derived quantities such as rotations) as

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026].
