an additional displacement degree of freedom. An alternative to this method, based on the enhanced assumed strain
concept (Simo and Rifai, 1990; Simo and Armero, 1992)
has been originally proposed by Büchter and Ramm (1992a)
and Büchter et al. (1994) and later on followed by various
authors (Betsch et al., 1995; Bischoff and Ramm, 1997;
Hauptmann and Schweizerhof, 1998; Wriggers et al., 1996,
and many others). Starting from a modified version of the
Hu–Washizu principle, this methodology can be understood
as a semidiscretization of the shell body in thickness direction on the basis of a multifield formulation, as opposed to
the usually applied standard displacement method.
Without going into technical details, it ought to be noted
for better comprehension of cross references to this method
in subsequent sections that the principal idea consists in
adding a strain component Ẽ 33 to the “compatible” strains
Eij = 12 (gij − Gij ), obtained from displacement derivatives,
E33 = 12 (g33 − G33 ) + Ẽ 33

(186)

where Ẽ 33 is assumed to be a linear function of the thickness
coordinate 𝜉 3 . The modified strain tensor reads
E = 12 (gij − Gij )Gi ⊗ Gj + Ẽ 33 G3 ⊗ G3

(187)

It can be deduced from the underlying variational principle that within a finite element formulation, the additional
unknown parameters associated with the interpolation of Ẽ 33
within the element can be eliminated on the element level
via static condensation; for details confer (Simo and Rifai,
1990). The global number of degrees of freedom per node
thus remains 6, which is considered the major advantage of
this formulation compared to the 7-parameter model based
on pure displacement assumptions mentioned in the previous
section. Both methods share the property that unmodified
three-dimensional constitutive laws can be used without
harming asymptotic correctness of the resulting theory.

5.6

Shell geometry and curvature

One fundamental challenge in the development of shell
theories is mapping of all involved quantities onto the
mid-surface while reducing the associated error to a
minimum. A crucial step in this dimensional reduction
is through-the-thickness integration of the strain energy.
Along with the shifter tensor Z = Gi ⊗ Ai , the integrand
contains contravariant base vectors in shell space, nontrivially depending on the shell curvature. A convenient
analytical integration for arbitrarily shaped shells is thus
precluded.

Geometrically exact shell models, based on the direct
approach, solve (or circumvent) this problem by defining the
strain tensor
Ê = Eij Ai ⊗ Aj = Z ⋅ E ⋅ ZT

(188)

referring to the contravariant base vectors on the mid-surface.
The influence of Z is thus concentrated on the material law,
in which the simple – though restrictive – assumption Z = G
facilitates an analytic expression for the material tensor that
is valid for arbitrarily curved shells. Note that
W int = 12 ET ∶ C ∶ E = 12 Ê ∶ Ĉ ∶ Ê = 12 Eij Cijkl Ekl

(189)

independently of Z due to the invariance of the energy
expression toward the bases of the involved tensor
components. As far as deformation energy is concerned,
assumptions for the shell shifter Z exclusively affect the
transformation of the infinitesimal volume element
dΩ = Z d𝜉 3 dΩ0

(190)

and the components of the material tensor Cijkl .
Attempts to conveniently approximate Z have been developed mainly in the context of explicit preintegration for
shell finite elements based on the degenerated solid approach
(Irons, 1973; Milford and Schnobrich, 1986; Stanley, 1985;
Zienkiewicz et al., 1973). One such possibility is a linear
series expansion
𝜕Z |
𝜉3
Z̃ = Z|𝜉 3 =0 + 3 ||
𝜕𝜉 |𝜉 3 =0

(191)

If the strain tensor Ê is used, these approximations are unnecessary. One either uses analytic preintegration along with
Z = G, or a numerical integration of the material law, for
which an approximation of Z is obsolete. An approximation
of the shell shifter comes into play implicitly in the latter
case. The subject of preintegration of the constitutive law is
covered in Section 5.8.

For the 5-parameter model developed herein, putting
modeling assumptions into concrete terms, we decide to
use the approximation of a constant shell shifter Z = G.
The resulting model is equivalent to the geometrically
exact shell formulation described in Section 4.3. Under
the provision of the assumption of strains varying linearly
through the thickness, this version of the 5-parameter
model turns out to be the most efficient one in terms of
the energy (Section 5.9).

5.7

Constitutive equations and stresses

Variation of stresses in thickness direction depends via the
material law to corresponding assumptions for the strains.
Assumptions for through-the-thickness stress distribution
and constitutive equations are thus closely related. Particularly in the context of displacement-based finite element
formulations, stresses and stress resultants are usually
obtained from the strains in a postprocessing step. In a more
general setting, for instance, using a multifield variational
framework for semidiscretization in thickness direction,
one might well have the possibility of arbitrarily deciding
about through-the-thickness distribution of individual stress
components.
In any case, it is crucial to establish a certain balance
between strain and stress distributions. Stress components
with no corresponding energetically conjugate strain tend to
produce artificial constraints and may lead to an over-stiff
behavior of the model.
Given the assumptions for displacements and strains
developed so far, the function spaces for the individual
strain components along a given transverse material fiber
(i.e., with 𝜉 1 and 𝜉 2 fixed) are

However, as we saw before, E33 ≡ 0 in our shell model and
thus this simple state of deformation cannot be represented
correctly, unless additional measures are taken. Graphically,
this constraint can be pictured as a rigid sleeve, surrounding
the plate under consideration and thus preventing its expansion in transverse direction, thus stiffening the configuration.
The usual procedure to eliminate this problem is static
condensation of transverse normal strains from the
material law by explicitly introducing the condition of
zero transverse normal stress. This can be easily accomplished for arbitrary anisotropic constitutive equations
(cf. equation (45)) as long as linear constitutive laws
are applied. For the sake of simplicity, we demonstrate
the procedure with the example of an isotropic material,
formulated in local Cartesian coordinates x̄ , ȳ , z̄ , where z̄
denotes thickness direction,

the material equations means an introduction of the
plane-stress condition, which is not quite correct. A state
of plane stress is defined as exclusively involving stresses
acting parallel to a certain plane. This is clearly not the
case in the context of shells. Koiter (1960) remarks that
for a shell model neglecting transverse shear strains, the
deformation energy involves only stresses acting parallel
to the shell’s mid-surface. In this context, the term plane
stress might enjoy some justification. Practical implementation of this modification for arbitrary coordinate
systems can be conveniently accomplished via equations
(137) and (138); see Sokolnikoff (1956).
For the 6-parameter model, first mentioned in Section
5.4, the situation is a little different. Here we have (again
assuming that the quadratic terms of the in-plane strains are
cancelled)
E𝛼𝛽 ∈ span{1, 𝜉 3 },

E𝛼3 ∈ span{1, 𝜉 3 },

E33a ∈ span{1}

(198)

and consequently
S𝛼𝛽 ∈ span{1, 𝜉 3 },
S33a ∈ span{1, 𝜉 3 }

S𝛼3 ∈ span{1, 𝜉 3 },
(199)

under the same conditions underlying the previous discussion of the 5-parameter model. Owing to the additional
displacement parameter, transverse shear and normal strain
spaces are enriched. Both strain and stress distributions are
now completely three dimensional up to order zero in 𝜉 3 ,
meaning that the aforementioned one-dimensional model
problem is now solved exactly. Moreover, the 6-parameter
model is asymptotically correct in membrane-dominated
problems.
There is, however, an unbalance between transverse normal
strains and stresses in the linear part. As can be seen from
equations (198) and (199), all strain and stress components except the transverse normal strain are completely
linear in 𝜉 3 . Consequently, a constraint similar to the one
described above is present, this time in the case of bending.
The resulting stiffening effect has been termed thickness
locking or Poisson thickness locking in the literature (Büchter
et al., 1994) and manifests itself in on overestimation of the
bending stiffness. Note that this error diminishes with neither
shell thickness nor mesh refinement in the context of finite
elements.
Manipulation of the material law by introducing a zero
transverse normal stress condition removes this constraint
but at the same time introduces artificial zero energy modes:

a pure thickness change with all other displacement components being identically zero produces zero energy because
the conjugate stress S33 to the only nonzero strain E33 is
eliminated. An alternative possibility is modification of the
material law after thickness integration, employing the aforementioned condensation only for the linear part of the transverse normal stress.
A much more popular method is to enhance the formulation to a 7-parameter model by additional strain or
displacement parameters, as described in Sections 5.4 and
5.5. The decisive advantage of this procedure is the fact
that unmodified three-dimensional constitutive laws can
be applied, which is particularly attractive for nonlinear
material modeling. Moreover, it facilitates to approximately
model three-dimensional stress and strain states, which is
advantageous in the presence of large strains.
In addition to the zero transverse normal stress condition,
it is customary in beam, plate, and shell theories to introduce
shear correction factors, thus implementing another modification of the material equations. Shear correction factors
are not necessary to obtain asymptotically correct models
but improve accuracy in the presence of transverse shear
deformations. The starting point for the derivation of the
most simple variant, that is, a constant shear correction factor
throughout the entire shell structure, neglecting the influence of loads, boundary conditions, and curvature, is the
energy density due to a given transverse shear force q̃ 𝛼 =

The material equations that have been postulated in the
course of deriving a 5-parameter shell model via the direct
approach in Section 4.3 adopt exactly the same idea, see
equation (117). For more details concerning derivation of
shear correction factors see, for instance, Noor and Burton
(1989, 1990) and references therein.
The 7-parameter model using 6 displacements and 1 strain
parameter, presented at the end of Section 5.5, induces
a linear variation of transverse shear strains and stresses.
The quadratic part is still missing and thus the mentioned
shear correction factor is needed (or is at least sensible)
as well. Moreover, a shear correction factor for the linear
part of the transverse shear strains, as proposed by Bischoff
(1999) (see also Bischoff and Ramm, 2000), can be derived
along the same lines. Assuming a cubic distribution of the
higher order transverse shear strains associated with linearly
varying thickness changes leads to a value 𝛽s = 7∕10 for the
corresponding entry in the material matrix. Similar to 𝛼s , this
reduces the deviation to the three-dimensional theory, especially in the range of thick plates and shells.
For a 7-parameter model exclusively based on displacements, quadratic transverse shear strains are obtained. If
these are not discarded, this supersedes application of the
shear correction factor 𝛼s for the linear part, while application of 𝛽s still makes sense. It ought to be remarked,
however, that the situation is different for general anisotropic
material laws because not all strain components are quadratically varying in 𝜉 3 , and artificial constraint stresses, in turn
varying quadratically in thickness direction, might occur.
This does not harm asymptotic correctness of the model for

33

both membrane- and bending-dominated situations, but the
quadratic accuracy in the shear terms is lost.
In general, implementation of orthotropic and general
anisotropic material laws is straightforward and does not
produce any problems or necessitate additional considerations. All formulae provided so far directly apply. The only
thing to change is the definition of the components Cijkl of
the material tensor.
Formulation of materially nonlinear formulations,
including elastic, hypo- or hyperelastic and plastic material
laws, damage, and so on, are not within the scope of the
present study (Simo and Hughes, 1998) for an up-to-date
reference). It is, however, worth a comment that there exist
two principal possibilities to implement such formulations,
namely, the direct expression of the nonlinear equations,
yield functions, and so on, in terms of stress resultants,
or a three-dimensional formulation, using stresses. The
latter approach is especially convenient when a 7-parameter
model is applied, because in this case the completely
three-dimensional, unmodified material equations can be
used. However, for conventional 5-parameter models also,
formulations based on stresses rather than stress resultants
are usually preferred because they tend to be more accurate.
For resultant-based formulations, see Crisfield (1979, 1995),
1976), Eidsheim (1980), and Skallerud and Haugen (1999).

5.8

Thickness integration of the material law

When deriving shell theories by the direct approach, the
two-dimensional material equations are postulated, albeit on
the basis of evidence from the three-dimensional theory. In
the case of derivation from three-dimensional continuum as
well as for shell elements based on the degenerated solid
approach, the material law is obtained from the integration of
the three-dimensional elasticity matrix. The corresponding
equations (47) and (80) are repeated here for convenience.
C = Cijkl Gi ⊗ Gj ⊗ Gk ⊗ Gl k,
Cijkl = 𝜆Gij Gkl + 𝜇(Gik G jl + Gil Gjk ),
ijkl

DK =

t∕2

∫−t∕2

(𝜉 3 )K Cijkl Z d𝜉 3

For flat plates, Z = 1 and Gij = Aij = const. in 𝜉 3 , and the
integration can trivially be carried out in advance. Analytic
expressions may well be obtained for special cases of
curved shells, like cylindrical shells, where relatively simply
formulae for through-the-thickness variations of Z and Gij
can be found.
In general, analytical integration of equation (80) is
impracticable unless certain simplifications are admitted.

In many cases, for instance, when nonlinear material laws
are used, analytical integration is not practical. Especially
in the context of a finite element implementation, the idea
of numerical integration of equation (205) arises quite naturally as an alternative to analytical thickness integration. It
also eliminates the necessity to introduce restrictive assumptions on through-the-thickness variation of the shifter Z (cf.
equation (191)). Numerical integration, however, in general
provides approximate solutions and thus certain assumptions
implicitly enter the shell formulation. These are sometimes
wrongly attributed to the numerical solution strategy (e.g.,
the finite element method); they are, however, an inherent
ingredient of the shell model (semidiscretization in thickness direction, Section 4). The corresponding errors do not
diminish with mesh refinement.
In principle, any numerical quadrature rule may be used
for thickness integration. In the context of the finite element
method, mostly Gauss–Legendre quadrature (also simply
called Gauss quadrature) is used because it has a good
ratio of accuracy to computational cost and it is capable to
exactly integrate polynomials. Sometimes, also closed form
Newton–Cotes formulae, for example, the Simpson integration rule, are used. Here, in contrast to Gauss integration,
quadrature points at both ends of the interval (i.e., at the top
on bottom surface of the shell) are involved. This may be
advantageous for stress recovery and in the context of materially nonlinear formulations.
Both Gauss integration and Newton–Cotes formulae are
able provide exact results for polynomials. Gauss integration
with n quadrature points exactly integrates polynomials of
order 2n + 1. Within the class of Newton–Cotes formulae,
the trapezoidal rule provides exact values for linear functions, and the Simpson rule exactly integrates third-order
polynomials. For higher order polynomials, the mentioned
Newton–Cotes integration rules only provide approximate
results.

The dependency of the strain components Eij of 𝜉 3 is predominantly driven by the order of the underlying displacement field. In the 7-parameter formulation, all displacement
components vary linearly through the thickness and the transverse normal strain field is enhanced by a linear term in 𝜉 3 .

structures because of the varying metric entering the definition of Eij , cf. equations (179) and (180).

Here, the order 8 in the denominator is exclusively driven by

emanates from the linear part of the transverse normal strain
along with the eighth-order term in C3333 and the quadratic
term in Z. For 5- and 3-parameter models, these orders are
smaller.
It is discussed next how many integration points are necessary for a sufficiently accurate numerical quadrature. In this
context, two different questions arise:
1.
2.

How many integration points are needed to obtain an
asymptotically correct model?
How many integration points are necessary to take into
account quadratic parts in the strain distribution and
higher order terms emanating from initial shell curvature?

The first question can be answered by analytical considerations. It is known that the simplest 3-parameter model
assuming the metric being constant through the thickness and neglecting quadratic strain terms (Love’s first
approximation) is asymptotically correct. In this case Z = 1
and the components Cijkl are constant in 𝜉 3 . With these
simplifications, the integrand is a quadratic function in 𝜉 3 ,
(Eij Cijkl Ekl Z) = (2). It has to be integrated exactly in
order to obtain an asymptotically correct model. When using
Gauss integration, two integration points are sufficient.
Within the class of Newton–Cotes schemes, at least the
Simpson rule has to be applied. Using the trapezoidal rule
provides only an approximation. The error can be reduced by
increasing the number of intervals, however, it will never be
exact. The numerical experiments below confirm that using
the trapezoidal rule for through-the-thickness integration
does not provide an asymptotically correct shell model. The
second question, raised above, is investigated with the help
of numerical examples in Section 5.9.
When materially nonlinear formulations such as
elastoplasticity or damage models are used, it is
important to capture the pronounced nonlinearities in
through-the-thickness distribution of the stress (see also
Section 7.1.4) such that more integration points are necessary than for the case of elasticity with smooth stress
distributions in thickness direction. It may also prove
sensible to switch from Gauss integration to integration
rules using sampling points at the upper and lower limits of
the area of integration, that is, 𝜉 3 = ±t∕2. These are potential

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

locations for maximum stress, and thus initial yielding. Due
to the fact that the yield condition is usually checked at the
integration points, a Gauss scheme tends to overestimate
plastic bending moments.

5.9

Numerical investigation of modeling errors

In this section, the consequences of some of the modeling
assumptions discussed above are investigated with the help
of numerical examples. In order to avoid influence from
discretization errors, this is not done on the basis of finite
element computations. Instead, a certain displacement field
for a given shell is prescribed. As an error measure, the
relative error of the deformation energy with respect to the
three-dimensional solution is used.
e=

Πint − Πint
3D

(220)

Πint
3D

is obtained from analytical inteThe reference solution Πint

gration in all spatial directions. Independent of the integration in thickness direction 𝜉 3 for the shell models (analytical
integration or numerical quadrature), integration in in-plane
direction (𝜉 1 and 𝜉 2 ) is always carried out analytically. This
guarantees that only influences from modeling assumptions
within the respective shell theories have an influence on the
error.
The geometry of the reference configuration for all
following examples is the cylindrical shell shown in Figure 5.
A cylindrical shell is chosen, because it allows for inextensional (pure bending) deformations. We use curvilinear
coordinates 𝜉 i along with the base vectors Ai for description
of the displacement field. The three-dimensional displacement field u and the displacement field v of the mid-surface

are thus given as
u = ui A1

In principle, any three-dimensional displacement field

strains and thickness change. However, in these cases, the
Kirchhoff-Love-type formulations, neglecting those effects,
would perform arbitrarily bad. Therefore, in order to be
able to assess certain phenomena also for the lower order
theories, only pure membrane and bending deformations
with zero transverse shear and normal strains are used.
The first scenario is a simple radial extension of the cylindrical shell, as shown in Figure 6. The second type of deformation is a pure bending deformation, shown in Figure 7.
In both the examples, geometrically linear kinematics is
assumed. For all configurations, the energy error is displayed
in diagrams as a function of shell thickness t.

5.9.1

Influence of assumptions for strains and initial
shell geometry

In this section, the influence of model assumptions
described in Sections 5.5, 5.6, and 5.7 are investigated.
In particular, these are the neglect of strain components Eij
quadratic in 𝜉 3 as well as simplifications concerning the
through-thickness-variation of the metric in the components
Cijkl of the material tensor C and the shifter Z in the definition of the infinitesimal volume element. The latter two
aspects exclusively concern the geometric description of the
undeformed shell.
Integration through the thickness is carried out analytically
in this Section in order to isolate the aforementioned aspects.
The effect of numerical quadrature in thickness direction,
theoretically addressed in Section 5.8, is investigated numerically in Section 5.9.2.
As for pure membrane deformations (radial extension,
Figure 6) the three-dimensional solution does not contain
quadratic strain terms, we focus on the pure bending deformation shown in Figure 7. The notation used to specify the
different models in the following diagrams is as follows:
C = exact representation of the varying metric in Cijkl ,
C0 = metric is assumed constant through the thickness
(Gij ≈ Aij ) in Cijkl ;
• Z = the exact shell shifter is used,
Z0 = the shifter is assumed constant through the thickness, Z ≈ 1;
• quad = quadratic strain components are taken into
account,
lin = quadratic strain components are neglected.
•

Thus, for instance, C0/Z0/lin denotes the simplest model,
assuming Z = G as well as Gij = Aij in the definition of
the components Cijkl of the material tensor. C/Z/quad would

37

be the most complex one, exactly taking into account all
geometric aspects. A confusion of the denomination “C0”
with C0 -continuity of functions should be excluded in the
given context.
The diagram in Figure 8 shows the error curves of seven
out of the eight possible combinations of C/C0, Z/Z0 and
quad/lin. The model C/Z/quad is not shown because it
provides the exact three-dimensional solution independently
of t such that the error is always zero. Note that the (red)
curves for C0/Z0/lin and C0/Z/lin are identical.
It is remarkable that within the group of models involving
linear strains, the version C0/Z0/lin provides the smallest
error. Recall that the strain components Eij refer to the tensor
̂ equation (66), and E = Z−1 ⋅ Ê ⋅ Z−T . Thus, information
E,
about shell geometry is implicitly contained in the definition
of the strain tensor. For the models of type C/Z0 and C0/Z
not even adding quadratic strain terms improves the situation.
The reason is the unbalance of geometric assumptions in the
material law and the shifter.
The model C0/Z0/quad exhibits a larger rate of convergence to the 3D solution with t → 0 than the models with
linear strains. This means that adding quadratic strain terms
is beneficial, as long as the assumptions for the material law
are balanced (i.e., either C0/Z0 or C/Z, the latter providing
the exact solution). On the other hand, when quadratic strain
terms are neglected, an improved representation of the geometry does not help, which can be deduced from the comparison of C0/Z0/lin and C/Z/lin. On the contrary, the “simpler”
version provides better results here, because the linear variation of strains is consistent with the assumption of a constant
metric. The convergence rates, however, are identical for all
models with linearly varying strains through the thickness.
As a final conclusion, a superiority in the significance of
the quadratic strain assumption can be observed. Adding
quadratic strains increases the order of convergence of the
balanced C0/Z0-version (cf. C0/Z0/quad vs. C0/Z0 lin).
Adding quadratic strains to the C/Z-version, with improved
representation of the geometry, provides the exact result
in this case. In simplified terms, one might say that the
“mechanical” assumption (through-the-thickness strain
distribution) is more important than the “geometrical” one
(initial curvature).

5.9.2

Influence of numerical integration through the
thickness

In order to isolate the effect of numerical quadrature in thickness direction for the numerical examples in this section,
all geometric terms in Cijkl and Z as well as quadratic
strain terms are included. In the notation of the previous
section, this means that only the model C/Z/quad is taken into
account, which provides the exact 3D-solution for the case of

analytical integration. The combination of different models
and types of integration is investigated in the following
section.
Three different types of integration are used, which are
denoted in the diagrams in Figures 9 and 10 as
•
•
•

Gauss n: Gauss–Legendre integration with n quadrature
points,
Simpson n: Simpson’s rule with n intervals,
trapezoidal n: trapezoidal rule with n intervals.

As all displacement fields vary linearly across the thickness, it can be expected that two-point Gauss quadrature
(Gauss 2) and Simpson integration with one interval
(Simpson 1) provide asymptotically correct models. For
comparison, the convergence curves for the thin-shell model
(C0/Z0/lin) using analytic integration are also added to the
following diagrams.
Numerical expense of the integration rules is proportional
to the number of quadrature points. For the trapezoidal rule
with 2 intervals, there are 3 quadrature points, and with 20
intervals, 21 quadrature points are used. Simpson integration
uses three quadrature points for the case of one interval; for
four intervals nine quadrature points are needed. For Gauss
integration, the number of integration points is directly given
by the corresponding rule. It is the most efficient one for
polynomials.
The diagram in Figure 9 shows the result for the example
of a cylinder subject to radial extension (Figure 6). It is a
membrane-dominated deformation for which the strains are
constant through the thickness in the thin limit. The integrand
of the expression for the internal energy is therefore also

constant in 𝜉 3 for t → 0. As all integration rules used here
are able to exactly integrate first-order polynomials, the error
tends to zero for all configurations.
The convergence rates, however, strongly depend on the
integration scheme. The trapezoidal rule with two intervals
shows the largest errors. Increasing the number of intervals
to 20 reduces the error, but it does not improve the rate
of convergence. A similar phenomenon is observed for the
Simpson rule.
It is interesting to note that the quadrature rules Gauss 2
and Simpson 1, which are able to exactly integrate third-order
functions lead to similar results as the analytically integrated
thin-shell model:
e(C/Z/quad/Gauss 2) ≈ e(C/Z/quad/Simpson 1)
≈ e(C0/Z0/lin/analytical)

(222)

This means that the additional model accuracy when taking
into account initial curvature and quadratic strain terms does
only pay off if higher order integration rules are used (Gauss
3 or higher).
The diagram in Figure 10 contains the corresponding
curves for the inextensional deformation shown in Figure 7.
It is a bending-dominated deformation and thus the strain
terms that are linear in 𝜉 3 are the leading terms in the
asymptotic limit t → 0. This leads to quadratic terms in the
expression for Πint , which have to be integrated exactly in
order to obtain an asymptotically correct result. It can be
clearly seen that the trapezoidal rule is unable to exactly
integrate quadratic functions and thus the error does not
tend to zero with t → 0, regardless of the number of intervals used. Apart from this, similar conclusions can be drawn

C0/Z0/lin/analytic.
To sum up, the following observations are made concerning
numerical thickness integration. The trapezoidal rule is not a
good choice for thickness integration. It leads to shell models
that are not asymptotically correct for bending. When taking
into account all terms emanating from initial curvature in the
material law Cijkl and the shifter Z as well as the parts of the
strains that are quadratic in 𝜉 3 , two-point Gauss integration

and Simpson’s rule with one interval provide a model accuracy that is similar to the exactly integrated model with
Gij ≈ Aij and neglecting quadratic strain components. This
means that such model fidelity is only sensible when higher
order integration schemes are used. In view of numerical efficiency, the only candidate for this is Gauss integration.

the case of pure bending (Figure 7). Various combinations of

Figure 11. Energy error for the case of pure bending, various combinations of shell models. (Reproduced with permission from Johannes
Irslinger. © 2013).

assumptions for geometry and strain distributions along with
different integration schemes are combined in the diagram in
Figure 11. For thickness integration, only the Gauss rule and
analytical integration are considered.
The model C0/Z0/lin using Gauss integration with at least
two quadrature points, not explicitly labeled in the diagram
in Figure 11, provides exactly the same results as obtained
with analytical integration (C0/Z0/lin/analytic, red curve),
because the integrand is a polynomial of order two in 𝜉 3 in
this case. Adding information about initial curvature of the
shell, but retaining the linear strain distributions through the
thickness (models of type C/Z/lin) yields the same convergence rate, regardless of the type of integration. The version
with analytical integration (green curve) even displays the
largest absolute error, confirming the remark at the end of
Section 5.9.1 that increasing the geometric fidelity does not
help if the mechanical assumptions (through-the-thickness
strain distribution) are too restrictive.
Adding the quadratic strain components to the model of
type C0/Z0, neglecting the terms for initial curvature (models
of type C0/Z0/quad), potentially increases the convergence
rates. Because in this case fourth-order terms appear in the
integrand, at least three Gauss quadrature points through
the thickness are needed. Higher order integration schemes
and analytical integration provide exactly identical results.
When using two-point Gauss integration, the absolute error
is slightly smaller than for the models with linear strains, but
the convergence rate is the same.
If the model is refined in both aspects – initial geometry and
strain distribution (model type C/Z/quad) – every extra Gauss
integration point increases the order of convergence (black
curves in Figure 11). As in this case fractions of rational

functions appear in the integrand, numerical integration will
never be exact, regardless of the number of quadrature points.
Recall that analytical integration in this case provides the
exact three-dimensional result. Again, two integration points
are not sufficient to take into account the additional model
fidelity.
In summary, the following conclusions can be drawn:
• If only two Gauss quadrature points are used, any refinement of the model (“mechanical” or “geometrical”) is
practically pointless.
• Adding quadratic strain terms along with at least three
Gauss quadrature points increases the order of convergence and significantly reduces the model error. More
than three Gauss points are not needed in this case.
• For the “full” model, taking into account the initial
curvature (refined geometry) and quadratic strain
terms (refined mechanics), each additional Gauss point
increases the order of convergence.
For an actual implementation, the models C0/Z0/lin/Gauss
2 and C/Z/quad/Gauss 3 are probably the most recommendable. For the case of plasticity, the slightly more expensive
Simpson integration, model C0/Z0/lin/Simpson 1 may be
recommendable because the quadrature points are located
at the top and bottom surface of the shell, where plastic
yield is initiated. Combining Simpson’s rule with the model
C/Z/quad is not sensible, because the integration order is too
low. Recall that Simpson integration will never go beyond the
convergence rates obtained with two-point Gauss integration
(Figures 9 and 10).

5.10

Degree of nonlinearity

Up to this point, we have dealt exclusively with the fully
geometrically nonlinear equations. For many applications,
this is unnecessary and a linearized theory sufficiently
reflects the actual behavior of the shell structure. Owing to
the special nature of shell problems, different approximation
stages, starting from the completely linearized theory up to a
fully nonlinear formulation have been developed in the past.
•

Linear shell theories use linearized strain measures
and thus consider equilibrium in the undeformed state.
Distinction between reference and current configurations
as well as different stress and strain measures are obsolete. These theories may be applied when deformations
are small and no instability phenomena are expected.
• Shell formulations for moderate rotations contain the
most important geometrically nonlinear terms, but not
all of them. The effect of respecting equilibrium in the
deformed state is taken into account approximately, but
rotation angles and strains are still assumed to be small.
They are valid for arbitrarily large rigid-body translations but only limited rotations. One important simplification is assuming sin 𝜙 ≈ 𝜙 and cos 𝜙 ≈ 1 for rotation
angles. With such models, buckling problems can be
solved approximately, however, in most snap-through
problems limiting of rotations will lead to unacceptable
deviations from the correct results.
• Shell formulations for large rotations include all geometrically nonlinear terms. Corresponding finite elements
are suited for analysis of shell problems involving arbitrarily large displacements and rotations, including buckling and snap through. The only remaining limitation is
strains being small. Note that this limitation is not due to
restrictions concerning kinematics, because there are no
such restrictions. All mathematical requirements such as
frame invariance or energy conservation in hyperelastic
problems are met. The restriction to small strains is owed
to the fact that the typical shell assumptions of neglecting
transverse normal strains and stresses may lead to considerable deviations from the three-dimensional solution in
the presence of large strains. Moreover, the use of a
St. Venant-Kirchhoff-type material might well preclude
physically meaningful results.
• Finally, shell theories including the effect of transverse
strains and stresses are valid for large strains and rotations. They not only involve the fully nonlinear representation of the strain tensor but additionally allow the
considering of completely three-dimensional constitutive
equations. It is important to remark that for the numerical results making sense in the case of large strains,
feasible material laws ought to be applied. The simple St.

41

Venant–Kirchhoff type for elastic materials used in most
parts of this study is not suited for the description of large
strains because it produces unrealistic results, especially
in the presence of compressive strains.
The somewhat peculiar distinction between moderate and
large rotations is of mostly historical value. Development of
shell theories of the former category was mainly motivated
by the wish to obtain such types of differential equations
that were easily (or at all) accessible to analytic solutions or
approximations based on series expansions. Given a formulation with finite elements, those considerations are obsolete.
Conceptually, the completely linearized theory and the fully
nonlinear theory are the simplest versions. They play the
most prominent role in modern shell finite elements.

5.11

Most important shell models

This section provides a brief overview of the most important shell models. Without going into details concerning
the mathematical formulation, the modeling assumptions
are specified with lead to some of the classical theories
in the spirit of Sections 5.4–5.8. The main concern is to
discuss consequences that these decisions may have on
model behavior, numerical analysis, and prospective finite
element formulations.

5.11.1

Membrane theory

Although not explicitly mentioned yet, and strictly speaking
not leading to what one calls “shell finite elements”,
membrane theory can be considered the lowest representative of a series of models to be discussed within this and the
following sections. In membrane theory, all strain and stress
components are assumed to be constant with respect to the
thickness coordinate. A director is therefore not needed, the
structural behavior is defined exclusively via in-plane strains
of the mid-surface. In this sense, membrane elements can be
considered as “curved plane-stress elements”.
Conceptually, the simplest approach is to consider the
actual body as being infinitely thin, such that the position
vectors to any point in the domain are identical to the position
vectors of the mid-surface.
X = R,

x = r,

The free parameters are the components ri of the mid-surface
displacement vector. The corresponding strain variables are

Anisotropic material equations may be obtained along the

Equations (224) and (225), along with the given material
law, implement particularly strong approximations because
they effectively remove bending moments and transverse
shear forces from the theory, thus restricting the problem
class to circumstances in which equilibrium is attainable with
membrane stresses only. Applying membrane theory therefore requires careful definition of loads and boundary conditions in order to avoid nonphysical solutions. For developable
shell geometries (flat plates, cylinders, and cones), the elastic
part of the system stiffness matrix will be singular, because
there is no stiffness toward inextensional deformations (pure
bending).
Technical membrane structures are usually prestressed.
The associated geometric stiffness provides some initial
resistance also to bending. In the context of dynamics, the
situation is even more relaxed – from a computational point
of view – due to consideration of inertia effects.

∫Ω

Membrane theory is useful for the computation of extremely
thin structures with high bending flexibility or when bending
resistance is assumed to be negligible for other reasons.
Typical applications are prestressed membranes in electronic
devices, airbags in automotive industry, tents made up of
woven fabrics, or folio in civil engineering.
It is important to distinguish between computation of the
membrane state in a shell and an actual membrane structure.
In the former case, membrane theory may provide simplifications compared to shell theory, which allow to obtain
closed form solutions for simple cases that reflect the most
important load-carrying behavior. This applies in particular
for axisymmetric cases, for example, cylindrical containers
subject to fluid pressure. Apart from bending layers, the
membrane solution can be practically identical to the shell
solution in such cases. In the latter case, there is practically
no resistance to compressive stresses because the structure
immediately buckles or wrinkles upon compression. Considerations of wrinkling effects is covered, for instance, in
Roddeman et al. (1987), Ziegler et al. (2000), and references
therein.

5.11.2

Kirchhoff-Love-type models

The first valid shell theory including bending but neglecting
the effect of transverse shear strains is usually attributed
to August E. H. Love and it is a straightforward extension
of the landmark contribution on plate theory by Gustav
Kirchhoff (Love, 1888; Kirchhoff, 1850); for the historical
development see Calladine (1988). The term Kirchhoff–Love
theory is often attributed to all sorts of shell formulations
including bending and excluding transverse shear strains. In
the mathematical literature, the name of Warner T. Koiter is
often used in a similar manner (Koiter, 1960).
The displacement assumption is obtained from

Note that v = [0 0 v3 ]T in the case of a linear theory
of plates. Along the supported edge the displacements
vanish identically, v ≡ 0, and thus v3,𝛼 = 0 holds at an edge
parallel to 𝜉 𝛼 . Owing to the aforementioned constraint, this
means that the corresponding rotation vanishes as well. The
normality assumption thus introduces an artificial constraint
on the twisting moments orthogonal to the edge, which is
physically not justified. As a consequence, transverse shear
forces are underestimated and artificial twisting moments
along a simply supported edge appear. This in turn means
that boundary layer effects cannot be described. Mathematically, this dilemma is caused by the fact that the governing
differential equation is of fourth order, thus allowing for two
independent boundary conditions only for the three degrees
of freedom available.
The procedure to correct this effect is one of the breakthrough original ideas in Kirchhoff’s landmark contribution
on plate theory: extra shear forces are computed from the
twisting moments via differentiation. The argument is based

5.11.3

Reissner-Mindlin-type models

The most popular shell model in the context of finite element
formulations is the one including transverse shear strains,
sometimes called first-order shear deformation theory
(FOSDT), but mostly associated with the names of Reissner
and Mindlin, owing to their pioneering works on shear
deformable plates (Reissner, 1945; Mindlin, 1951). Both
formulations are not exactly identical, and moreover, have
in fact not been the only works on this subject in the time
period of their publication (see, for instance, Bollé, 1947;
Hencky, 1947). Their works have in common, though, the
principal kinematic assumptions, allowing rotations being
independent from the mid-surface deformations. The term
Reissner–Mindlin kinematics seems to be justified for this
reason.
The displacement assumption is the same as in the case of
Kirchhoff–Love models, equation (232),
u = v + 𝜉 3 𝛿d = v + 𝜉 3 (𝚲 − G) ⋅ D

(249)

In contrast to the formulation described in the previous
section, the normality condition equation (233) is omitted,
thus allowing for transverse shear deformations. The undeformed configuration is uniquely defined by specifying R and
D, where the natural choice for the director field is the set
of normal vectors on the surface described by the position
vectors R, as already indicated in equation (17).
Computation of base vectors and metric tensors on the midsurface and in the shell body follows along the same lines as
put forward in the previous sections. Omitting the quadratic
terms of the strain components with respect to the thickness
coordinate 𝜉 3 , the resulting strain distributions are

near the edges, the picture is completely different for
shear-deformable models. Here, twisting moments have to
be zero at the edge and there is a steep gradient toward the
edge in a thin boundary layer in which the twisting moments
approach zero.
In the same direction, transverse shear forces start building
up. The difference between the shear forces corresponding
to the support reactions obtained directly at the edges to the
ones in the domain near the edge (but outside the boundary
layer) exactly complies to the effective shear forces of the
Kirchhoff model. Moreover, negative transverse shear force
concentrations at corners of rectangular plates mimic the
concentrated forces discussed before. In fact, for the asymptotic limit of vanishing plate thickness t → 0, these forces
become singular for the Reissner–Mindlin model.

5.11.4

Layer-wise and higher order models

A straightforward extension of the series starting with
Kirchhoff–Love models and continuing with Reissner–
Mindlin formulations, including transverse shear strains,
are the previously mentioned 7-parameter models (Section
5.4). Additionally introducing transverse normal strains,
they represent the lowest possible models being feasible for
implementation of unmodified three-dimensional constitutive laws. Rössle et al. (1998) presented a proof of
asymptotic correctness of a corresponding plate theory.
Motivation for development of plate and shell models
taking into account effects of even higher order is twofold:

distribution through the thickness (Babuşka and Li, 1991;
Babuşka et al., 1992; Lo et al., 1977; Schwab, 1996; Düster
and Rank, 2000 among others). As more than one director
vector is associated with such formulations, they are also
called multi-director models (Figure 12). With such formulations, for instance, three-dimensional boundary layers near
edges may be resolved. The most important group of such
formulations are called hierarchical models because they
inherently contain a hierarchy of plate or shell models (Actis
et al., 1999). The term hierarchic contains exactly the same
notion as in structural finite elements, used for instance, in
the context of adaptive p-methods: the function spaces (here,
for through-the-thickness interpolation of displacements) are
spanned by a hierarchical basis, each additional base function
being orthogonal to, or at least linearly independent of, the
previous ones.
Note that, for instance, standard Lagrange polynomials are
neither hierarchic nor orthogonal. Increasing the order of
interpolation necessitates provision of a complete new set
of shape functions in this case. Using simple monomials
already leads to a hierarchic structure, however, the individual base functions are not orthogonal. For the case of
plates, a set of orthogonal basis functions is provided by
Legendre polynomials.

Hierarchic models are subject to two fundamental conditions. Firstly, each individual member of the hierarchy has to
recover the three-dimensional elasticity solution in the thin
limit
(257)
lim(uNmodel − u3d ) = 0 ∀N ≥ 0
t→0

where uNmodel denotes the solution obtained with an Nth
order model. Secondly, for any given thickness t, the
series of model solutions has to converge toward the exact
three-dimensional one,
lim (uNmodel − u3d ) = 0

N→∞

∀t≥0

(258)

It has to be mentioned that the solution u must be represented
in a feasible norm to fulfill these requirements. Also in the
thin limit, the model solutions will not represent every detail
of the three-dimensional solution, like stress singularities
(Figure 13).
Especially in the case of plates, it may be sensible to
choose different orders of approximation for in-plane
and out-of-plane displacements. A typical denomination of such models specifies the polynomial orders of
the two in-plane directions and the thickness direction

Formulations of group B are especially well suited for
modeling of laminated and sandwich structures. Subdivision into layers is indicated by the actual physical setup
of the structure under consideration. Displacement interpolations have to satisfy C0 -continuity in between individual layers (Figure 13), thus naturally reflecting the actual
physical behavior of such structures (in contrast to the
C∞ -continuous hierarchical models). Formulations of this
kind are called multi-layer or layer-wise models (Braun et al.,
1994; Epstein and Huttelmaier, 1983; Gruttmann, 1996;
Reddy, 1984a, 1984b, 1987, 1997) and Computational
Modeling of Damage and Failures in Composite Laminates).
For parametrization of multi-layer shell models, it is
self-suggesting to use individual directors for each layer.
Corresponding finite elements can thus be viewed as
sequentially stacked shell elements. Formulations exist with
layer-wise Kirchhoff–Love, Reissner–Mindlin, or higher
order kinematics, including transverse normal strains and
stresses. The latter are especially attractive for materially
nonlinear computations, taking into account interlaminar
stresses and possible effects of delamination.

6 INSTABILITY, BUCKLING AND
SNAP-THROUGH
6.1

Preliminary remarks

The issue of instability has to be considered for any structural system under compression. It is, however, particularly
pronounced for thin-walled structures, such as beams, plates,

and shells with their typically optimized behavior maximizing the stiffness-to-weight ratio. More than any other type
of structures, thin shells tend to suddenly fail due to buckling under certain circumstances, even if prior to such failure,
in the prebuckling range, deformations have been small and
material behavior was still elastic. Particularly shells are
notorious troublemakers in this respect because they tend to
seemingly unpredictable sudden catastrophic failure due to
instability phenomena.
Structural stability is a scientific discipline in its own right
that cannot be treated exhaustively in this section. The interested reader is referred to the text books by Timoshenko and
Gere (1961), Britvec (1973), Bazant (2010), and Wriggers
(2008) as well as the chapter Buckling. Instead, we will try to
shed some light on the most important phenomena and analysis methods in the context of buckling and snap through of
shells under quasi-static loading. Buckling and snap-through
phenomena are intrinsically dynamic processes in the postcritical regime. Dynamic stability in the sense of stability of
dynamic processes, however, is a different issue, which is not
treated here.

6.2

Basic phenomena and definitions

For further discussion, the idea of a static equilibrium
path has to be introduced. This concept lends itself to
graphical representation of nonlinear structural behavior in
the form of response diagrams. The most widely used form of
these diagrams is the load–displacement response diagram,
Figure 14.
A static equilibrium path is defined as the locus of all points
in an abstract geometrical space for which the static equilibrium conditions are satisfied. For a system with ndof degrees
of freedom and one independent load parameter 𝜆 it is a curve
in an (ndof + 1)-dimensional hyperspace. The curves shown
in Figure 14 are a projection into one particular plane of
this space. Such two-dimensional load–displacement curves
thus only tell a rather small part of the complex story of
the real structural response. As static equilibrium paths are

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

Models and Finite Elements for Thin-Walled Structures

λ

Postcritical regime
Critical
points

Secondary path

Fundamental
(primary) path

Tertiary path
u

Figure 14. Static equilibrium paths and critical points.

often used to illustrate key concepts of nonlinear structural
behavior and stability, special care has to be taken in view of
this restriction.
Moreover, the interpretation of equilibrium paths as
load–displacement curves in the sense of a time-dependent
response diagram reflecting an experimentally verifiable
process is not always appropriate. It crucially depends on
the type of controlling parameter (e.g., load, displacement,
strain, volume, or energy control), from which conclusions
can be drawn from a given static equilibrium path concerning
the actual structural behavior. For many independent loads,
different load parameters may be relevant, distinguishing,
for instance, dead load (self weight) and life load (service
loads).
All tools for numerical analysis of nonlinear structural
behavior are based on the idea of solving the nonlinear structural equations only at a discrete number of “snap shots”, for
instance, for different load levels. A certain control parameter, for instance, the load level 𝜆, is incrementally increased
and the nonlinear governing equations are solved iteratively
to detect the next state of equilibrium (incremental-iterative
procedure). A classical procedure starts with a linearization,
using, for example, Newton’s method as iteration scheme (cf.
Section 7.5).
The equilibrium path that crosses the initial state, often also
used as reference state, is called the primary (fundamental)
equilibrium path or, for short, the primary path. It extends
from the initial state up to special states of equilibrium called
critical points. They will be discussed later. A path that is not
a primary path but is connected with it at a critical point is
called a secondary path (and so forth with tertiary, etc. paths).
There may also be isolated equilibrium paths, not connected
with any other path or the undeformed state.
As already mentioned, certain points of an equilibrium path
have special significance in applications. Of major interest

to our subject are critical points such as bifurcation points,
limit points, and turning points. In this context, it is crucial
to take into account how the structural response is controlled:
by load, displacement, strain, or with a mixed control.
Structures under gravity load follow a load control (or
force control) mechanism. Laboratory experiments are often
conducted by displacement control of a selected displacement parameter. In real applications driven by load control
(e.g., from service loads or dead load), smaller structural
elements may well be driven in the sense of a displacement control by the larger surrounding stiff parts. Their
deformation acts as a (prescribed) constraint for the smaller
substructures. Strain control in an experiment guides the
loading device via a strain gauge. Mixed load and displacement control prescribes load and displacement parameters in
a certain combination. Structures may also be controlled by
a certain amount of energy or by volume control in the case
of pressure loading.
Critical points are of utmost engineering significance due
to the special physical characteristics of structural behavior
in their vicinity. The diagram in Figure 14 shows two types of
critical points, namely a bifurcation point and a limit point.
A bifurcation point is characterized by the fact that two
or more equilibrium paths meet. Equilibrium points on
the primary path beyond a bifurcation point are unstable
(the corresponding line in Figure 14 is not marked as
“postcritical” in this case, in order to be able to illustrate
the limit point, occurring later on that path). In a stable
bifurcation, equilibrium points on the secondary path are
stable. Thus, in an experiment, the structure will follow this
path. If also the secondary path is unstable, the mechanical
behavior usually switches from (quasi-) static to dynamic.
The actual behavior strongly depends on the control parameter. Buckling phenomena are the most prominent examples
for bifurcations.
At limit points, the tangent to the equilibrium path is horizontal, that is, parallel to the displacement axis. If the structure is further loaded by load control, no static equilibrium
exists near by so that the structure dynamically snaps through
into a postcritical position. In practical situations, often the
amount of elastic energy transferred to kinetic energy in such
situations is so large that the structure collapses, mostly by
material failure. A mathematical characterization of limit
points and bifurcation points is provided later.
Turning points are points at which the tangent to the equilibrium path is vertical, that is, parallel to the load axis.
They may also be called critical points once the structure is
controlled by the related displacement component. It can be
seen in Figure 15 that displacement control fails at the point
with the vertical tangent. It is the same situation as for a limit
point in the case of load control (Figure 16).

6.3

Instability and determination of critical points

Let us next try to define the notions of stability, instability
and their relationship to critical points. A physically intuitive
concept may be stated as follows:
“A structure is stable at an equilibrium position if it returns to
that position upon being perturbed by an extraneous action.”

A popular illustration of this concept, probably first
presented by Timoshenko and Gere (1961) is reproduced
in Figure 17. The ball on Figure 17(a) is in a stable
state of equilibrium, because it is in a state of minimum
potential energy. External work has to be invested to
move it away from its position. Upon a small perturbation, it will immediately return to its initial position.
The ball in Figure 17(c) will obviously not return. The
potential energy level is a local maximum and energy

λ

is released upon perturbation. This equilibrium position is therefore unstable and highly sensitive to even
small perturbations. Figure 17(b) shows an intermediate
state, sometimes called “neutrally stable” or “indifferent”.
Within a process of static loading this illustrates the transition between stable and unstable behavior at a critical
point.
This point of view is simple, illustrative, and satisfactory for practical considerations. It can also be applied to
areas outside structural mechanics, such as economy or
psychology. It is closely related to the so-called static criterion, which states that a critical point is reached if besides the
original equilibrium position (fundamental state) an infinitesimally adjacent equilibrium position (neighboring state) at
the same load level exists.
For a rigorous description of instability phenomena, this
criterion must be expressed in mathematical terms. Let d𝚽
be the difference vector between the current state and the
neighboring state, then for a discrete (or discretized) system
with tangent stiffness K it holds that
K ⋅ d𝚽 = 𝟎

(259)

in this case, because the transition between both states
requires zero force. A nontrivial solution of equation (259)
(i.e., a solution for which d𝚽 ≠ 𝟎) requires the tangent stiffness to be singular
Det (K) = 0

(260)

This means that the tangential stiffness matrix is crucial to
judge the state of a system. The determination of critical
points is closely related to the analysis of the singularities
of K.

One remarkable result of this analysis is the fact that for the
case of bifurcation, the buckling mode is orthogonal to the
load vector. This is in line with the mechanical characteristics of shell buckling, which typically involves a sudden
change of the deformation pattern, usually switching from
a membrane state to a state of bending. The bending deformations typically occur in the shape of multiple buckles. For
instance, an axially compressed cylinder develops a series of
inward and outward buckles around its entire circumference
upon buckling.

6.4

Instability behavior of thin-walled structures

Consideration of instability phenomena is not restricted
to identifying a critical load at which a bifurcation or
snap-through point is reached. Knowledge about the postcritical behavior is crucial for assessment of stability
behavior and safety of the structure. In order to explain
the characteristic instability phenomena of thin-walled
structures, a lineup of beams, plates, and shells is considered next, describing the typical behavior in the elastic
range.

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. Beams exhibit a mostly neutral behavior after passing a
critical point (Euler buckling load).
Plates are typically good natured in the postcritical range
and often allow further increase of the load.
Shells may behave extremely malicious in the postcritical range, often showing a sudden and extreme drop in
stiffness and load bearing capacity.

Figure 18 shows a typical load–displacement curve for
beam-column buckling (Euler beam). The postbuckling
branch is initially horizontal with a slight increase for very
large longitudinal deformations. Buckling occurs if the

system does not return to its initial state of equilibrium
upon a small perturbation. For a beam, the corresponding
resistance is due to bending. Also the postbuckling stiffness
is dominated by bending. The imperfect (real, experimental)
load–displacement curve does not differ significantly
from the perfect one. Beams are usually not sensitive to
imperfections. In spatial truss and frame structures, the
global structural behavior may well be different, that
is, more sensitive to imperfections and unstable in the
secondary path.
The mechanism initiating buckling of flat panels (plates),
schematically shown in Figure 19, is essentially the same as

Figure 20. Buckling mechanism of a shell.

for beams. Bending stiffness is the crucial measure for the
critical load. However, if all edges of the plate are supported,
bending moments in two spatial directions and twisting
moments are activated by an infinitesimal perturbation.
In the postbuckling regime, the evolving behavior is
different than for beams. Due to the double curvature of
the buckling deformation, membrane forces help to resist
postbuckling deformation for the assumed boundary conditions (Figure 19b). Material fibers orthogonal to the load
direction are in tension and thus help carrying the load
like a cable carries the deck of a suspension bridge. The
postbuckling branch of the load displacement curve is
therefore steeper than the one of a one-dimensional beam.
Similar to beams, plates are not particularly sensitive to
imperfections.
The buckling mechanism of shells depends on geometry, loads, and boundary conditions and it is much
more complex than for beams and plates. It is explained
exemplarily for the case of an axially loaded cylinder,
exhibiting a diamond-shaped buckling pattern. The
load–displacement curve is schematically shown in
Figure 20(a) and a parallel section of a possible buckling
pattern in Figure 20(b).
In contrast to beams and plates, a shell activates membrane
forces already for infinitesimally small deformations (i.e.,
perturbations). This means that the resistance to initial buckling depends on the membrane stiffness rather than the
bending stiffness. Consequently the ideal buckling load may
be orders of magnitude higher than for a flat plate with the
same thickness, because membrane stiffness is much larger
than bending stiffness for thin-walled structures.
From the schematic sketch of the buckling pattern in
Figure 20(b), it can be seen that outward buckles lead to

tensile forces in hoop direction whereas inward buckles
lead to compression. For infinitesimally small buckles, the
corresponding hoop forces work against the deformation. In
the postbuckling range, however, the buckles have a finite
size – like the ones shown Figure 20 along with a sign change
in the curvature. Consequently, the compressive forces in the
inward buckles exhibit a destabilizing effect. The local structural behavior of the inward buckles is comparable to a snap
through of a flat arch or shallow shell. This is the explanation
for the sudden drop of the load-carrying capacity immediately after buckling. In fact, as the buckling load is reached,
the shell instantaneously jumps into the state of deformation
shown in the picture in a dynamic process. In practical cases,
this is often accompanied by material nonlinear effects or
even material failure such that the structure collapses immediately. In particular, for load control an immediate collapse
has to be expected.

6.5

Classical and linear prebuckling analysis

Tracing a nonlinear equilibrium path until the first critical
point is reached and identified can be computationally expensive and sometimes may even fail due to nonconvergence
of the Newton algorithm within a certain path following
strategy. The load level of critical points, however, can also
be found approximately with the help of linear or classical
prebuckling analysis in certain cases.
Classical and linear prebuckling analysis rely on the
assumption that the structural response prior to buckling is
essentially linear or at least approximately linear and that the
displacements and rotations before reaching a bifurcation
point are so small that a linear analysis is justified. There
are also cases where these prerequisites do not hold and

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

52

still prebuckling analysis provides reasonable results (and
vice versa); however, these special cases are not further
pursued here.
If the aforementioned requirements are met, prebuckling
analysis may be expected to yield acceptable predictions
for critical loads in many cases. The so-called Euler
cases in buckling of beam-columns is a typical example.
In general, only bifurcation points can be identified by
prebuckling analysis, because the prebuckling range in
the case of snap-through problems is typically highly
nonlinear.
In the absence of load stiffness effects, the tangent stiffness
matrix for a given load factor can be written as
K(𝜆) = K e + K u (𝜆) + K g (𝜆)

(266)

Here, K e is the elastic stiffness, that is, the stiffness matrix of
the linear problem. K u and K g are the initial displacement
stiffness and geometric stiffness, respectively. The former
takes into account stiffness changes due to change of geometry, the latter results from prestressing effects and is therefore also often denoted as initial stress stiffness.
Prebuckling analysis starts from the idea of linearizing
equation (266).

Approximating the actual tangent stiffness by the linearized

the tangent stiffness being a linear function of the load
parameter, which is justified given an approximately linear
prebuckling range. It can be used to check the criterion for
a critical point in a simplified manner by solving a generalized eigenvalue problem, as shown below. If both nonlinear
contributions from K u and K g are taken into account, this is
called linear prebuckling analysis.
As the deformations prior to buckling are typically
quite small, but the involved stresses are not, usually
the initial displacement stiffness effects are negligible,
Det(K u ) ≪ Det(K g ). This further simplification gives rise to
classical prebuckling analysis with the approximation

is obtained. The linearized geometric stiffness matrix K LIN

is practically computed by a geometrically linear analysis of
the system for a unit load 𝜆 = 1 and evaluating the geometric
stiffness on the basis of the corresponding state of stress.
The lowest eigenvalue 𝜆1 is an estimate for the critical load
factor, and the corresponding eigenvector 𝚽1 indicates the
expected buckling mode. A classical prebuckling analysis
often overestimates the critical load. If the real prebuckling
behavior is linear, the exact critical load factor is obtained.
Obviously, the computational cost for such a prebuckling
analysis is much smaller than for a fully nonlinear analysis
because it only involves one linear analysis and the solution
of an eigenvalue problem. Hence, it has great practical significance. However, certain limits of applicability have to be
respected. Important limitations are as follows:
• Displacements at the critical point have to be small
enough to justify neglecting the initial displacement stiffness.
• In the prebuckling range, the relationship between the
loads and the structural response, in particular stresses,
has to be approximately linear.
• Loads have to be independent of the displacements (this
is a prerequisite for validity of the static criterion).
• Material behavior has to be elastic prior to buckling.
• The effects of imperfections are not properly taken into
account, even if prebuckling analysis of imperfect structures is performed!
The last restriction is probably the most important one for
practical interpretation of the results of buckling analyses.
This applies also to nonlinear stability analyses, treated in
the following section. The third restriction is, for instance,
violated in classical prebuckling analysis of shell buckling subject to external pressure. The pressure load being a
follower force gives rise to significant load stiffness effects,
which are ignored by both linear and classical prebuckling
analyses. The corresponding results are on the unsafe side.
For instance, the buckling load of a cylindrical shell subject

6.6

Nonlinear stability analysis

The static criterion, equation (260), provides a number of
opportunities to identify critical points during the course of
a nonlinear analysis. By monitoring the determinant of the
tangential stiffness matrix, the critical point can be identified
as the first one for which the determinant becomes zero. A
typical problem with this method is that the point where
Det(K) = 0 will in general not be found exactly within an
incrementally iterative procedure. The observation is then
a sign change in the determinant. If, however, more than
one critical point is passed within one step, the sign may or
may not change, depending on the number of critical points
passed (sign change for an odd number, no sign change for
an even number). Therefore, in problems with many degrees
of freedom, simply observing the determinant of the stiffness
matrix is in general not adequate.
The solution of the linear system of equations in every load
step is often accomplished using a triangular decomposition
of the tangential stiffness
K = L ⋅ D ⋅ LT

(271)

where D is a diagonal matrix. Its entries are all positive in
a stable prebuckling branch. After passing critical points,
negative diagonal entries Dii < 0 show up. The number of
negative entries is equal to the number of critical points
passed. It keeps increasing even if Det(K) > 0 and thus
observing Dii is more practical than looking at the determinant of the stiffness matrix.
An estimate for the critical load factor 𝜆crit may also
be obtained from an accompanying eigenvalue analysis.
It corresponds to a prebuckling analysis starting from the
current equilibrium position at a certain load level 𝜆̂ rather
than from the undeformed state. For a total Lagrange formulation, this leads to the generalized eigenvalue problem
(Brendel and Ramm, 1980; Helnwein and Mang, 1997)
(K|𝜆=𝜆̂ + 𝜆(K g + K u )|𝜆=𝜆̂ ) ⋅ 𝚽 = 𝟎

(272)

The lowest eigenvalue 𝜆 is the relative critical load factor
̂ As in prebuckling
with respect to 𝜆̂ such that 𝜆crit ≈ 𝜆𝜆.
analysis, the corresponding eigenvector 𝚽 is an estimate of
the buckling mode. A series of linear eigenvalue analyses at
different load levels leads to an eigenvalue curve eventually
intersecting the load–displacement response at the bifurcation point.

Probably, the most sophisticated type of nonlinear stability
analyses are the so-called direct methods, which are able
to exactly determine the critical points. One of the most
prominent direct techniques relies on extended systems. The
principal idea is to extend the system of equations by additional information that describes the instability. The solution
of this extended system of equations is a point which is both
an equilibrium point and a critical point at the same time.
At limit points and single bifurcation points, the tangent
stiffness matrix K has one zero eigenvalue along with an
eigenvector 𝚽 satisfying the condition K ⋅ 𝚽 = 𝟎. A corresponding extended system of equations is

By evaluating the scalar product 𝚽T ⋅ Fext,0 , it can be
checked whether the critical point is a limit or a bifurcation point. This is important because upon convergence, the
system of equations (274) loses rank for the case of bifurcation points. In fact, the method described so far does only
work in the case of limit points. The reason for the rank
deficiency is related to the fact that the tangent stiffness K
is singular at the critical point. Thus, the ndof equations in
the first set of rows in the coefficient matrix become linearly
dependent. The entry −Fext,0 in the last column does not
add to linear independency, because for the case of a bifurcation point, it is orthogonal to the buckling mode 𝚽 (cf.
equation (265)) and thus orthogonal to the eigenvector that
belongs to the zero eigenvalue of K.

equations (273) therefore has to be enhanced by an additional
equation (Wriggers and Simo, 1990).

Looking at equation (276), one might ask the question
whether the additional term changes the physics of the
problem. After all, a force in the direction of 𝚽 is added
to the equilibrium equation, which is in reality not present.
However, due to the property of 𝚽 being the direction of
buckling, there is no stiffness in this direction and equilibrium is only possible if this artificial stabilizing force is zero.
In fact, 𝜓 converges to zero and thus the method is both stable
and consistent.
It can be checked in advance by a prebuckling analysis or
an accompanying eigenvalue analysis whether a limit point
or a bifurcation point has to be expected. Thus, a practical
procedure while applying the technique of extended system
is as follows:
1.

3.

Use a path-following scheme to trace the primary path
until certain indicators (e.g., Det(K) or Dii ) suggest that
there is a critical point closely ahead.
Estimate the expected type of critical point, for instance,
by an accompanying eigenvalue analysis.
Starting from this “nearly critical” point, use the feasible
version of extended system (equation (274) for a limit
point, equation (277) for a bifurcation) to exactly
compute the critical load factor 𝜆crit , the buckling mode
𝚽, and the corresponding deformation state D.

Structural safety of thin-walled structures in the context
of buckling cannot be assessed without taking into account
the postbuckling behavior. Apart from a fully nonlinear
analysis, treated in the following section, Koiter’s initial
postbuckling analysis, proposed in his Ph.D. thesis from
1945 (see Koiter, 1967 for an english translation), provides
a technique to estimate the characteristic behavior immediately after a bifurcation point has been reached. Via a
