Restart a path-following technique, using as predictor
step an estimate for the direction of the secondary path.
This may be the buckling vector 𝚽 available from detection of the critical point, or a result from Koiter’s initial
postbuckling analysis.

Further path following, for example, using an arc length
method does not differ from the one used on the primary
path. Theoretically, with this technique the entire network
of equilibrium paths of a structural system may be
determined.

6.7

Sensitivity to imperfections

Real structures usually do not enjoy the perfect features
assumed in their mathematical or numerical analysis. This
concerns geometry and material properties as well as loads
and boundary conditions. In most cases, little is known about
imperfections, which is why their influence on structural
behavior is hard to predict. Often, predictions are possible
only with respect to statistical properties of the structural
response. For imperfect thin-walled structures, the actual
buckling load may be much smaller than the predicted, theoretical, ideal buckling load.
One of the reasons for the pronounced imperfection sensitivity of shells is the characteristic shape of the postbuckling branches. Figure 16(a) shows a schematic sketch of an
equilibrium path, which is, for instance, typical for buckling of axially compressed cylinders. The primary path,
starting from the origin of the coordinate system, is almost
straight. It is plotted until the first bifurcation point. In the
case of an experiment with displacement control, the load
suddenly drops to a significantly lower level, prescribed by
the curved secondary branch, followed by jumping into other
modes during further increase of displacement loading (mode
jumping). However, already in earlier stages, the distance
between the primary path and the secondary path is rather
small. The small geometric distance in the load displacement diagram corresponds to a small “energetic distance”.

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

Models and Finite Elements for Thin-Walled Structures

Models and Finite Elements for Thin-Walled Structures

This means that a perturbation (e.g., a local pinching force
in radial direction) introducing a small amount of energy
into the system may cause a jump from the (stable) primary
path to the (unstable) secondary path. For initially imperfect
structures, this energetic vicinity of primary and secondary
paths may cause a severe drop of the experimentally observed
buckling load compared to the ideal buckling load. This is
also indicated in the diagram in Figure 20.
In building codes and practical design rules, the ideal buckling loads are therefore multiplied by knock-down factors,
which may be significantly smaller than 1.0. One strategy
to be on the safe side is to take the lowest load level on
the secondary path as design load. This corresponds to the
analysis of a bending- dominated, quasi-inextensional postbuckling mode according to Croll (1975). For load factors
𝜆 below this level, there is only one state of equilibrium,
namely on the primary path, regardless of the magnitude of
imperfections and the vulnerability of the shell to imperfections.
Not all types of shells exhibit this type of behavior, though.
HP shells (hyperbolic paraboloids), for instance, may be
stable in the postcritical range. The reason is that due to
negative Gaussian curvature, there are also material fibers in
tension that stabilize the shell.
The actual imperfections of a given shell can hardly be
known before it is built. Even after that they may be hard
to measure or assess (e.g., load imperfections). Feasible
assumptions or worst case scenarios have to be applied. The
question which imperfection would be the most dangerous
one, however, is still an open issue. One possibility is to use
a buckling mode 𝚽 or a statistical composition of several
modes 𝚽i , obtained from prebuckling analysis as geometric
imperfection.
It may also be desirable to assess the ideal buckling load
of the perfect shell by nonlinear stability analysis. This can
be done with the help of extended systems, as described in
the previous section. In many commercial codes, however,
this technique is not available. In this case, numerical imperfections have to be introduced in order to avoid that the
path-following scheme follows the (unstable) primary path
beyond all critical points. As the academic problem statement is to find the theoretical, ideal buckling load, the numerical imperfections do not have to be related to the real,
expected imperfections. The guidelines for a proper choice
of numerical imperfection are as follows:
•
•
•

They ought to be small enough, not to change the
problem too much.
They ought to be big enough to trigger buckling.
They ought to be general enough in order to ensure
that the first bifurcation point is found. In particular,

this means that all symmetries of the system have to be
removed.
For snap-through problems, numerical imperfections are
not necessary, because there is only one equilibrium path
anyway. As a matter of fact, adding imperfections in general
converts a bifurcation point to a limit point. This can, for
instance, be seen from the curve for the imperfect shell in
Figure 20. Checking the criterion from equation (265) in
this case yields 𝚽T ⋅ Fext,0 ≈ 0. This means that the buckling mode is no longer exactly orthogonal to the load vector,
as it is the case for the bifurcation point of the perfect shell.
However, 𝚽T and Fext,0 ≈ 0 are almost orthogonal, indicating
that the mechanical characteristics is essentially that of a
bifurcation. The near orthogonality is a quantitative measure
for the amount of influence of the numerical imperfection.

7

FINITE ELEMENT FORMULATION

Theory and applications of finite element methods are
covered in Spectral Methods and Mixed Finite Element
Methods. The discussion in this section therefore focuses
on special implications of models for thin-walled structures with respect to their usage within the context of the
finite element method. Moreover, concrete derivations are
restricted to standard displacement formulations.
As already mentioned earlier, it is beyond the objective of the present contribution to review the multitude of
different finite element formulations available in the literature. Moreover, scientific papers on plate and shell finite
elements often represent a combination of contributions on
both modeling in the sense of Section 5 and discretization.
In this chapter, the attempt is made to carefully distinguish
between these substantially different steps. This means that
in the following the focus is on discretizations for the specific
models discussed in the previous chapter.
For smooth shells, using splines as shape functions may
be advantageous compared to the commonly used Lagrange
polynomials. In particular, using B-Splines or NURBS
allows higher continuity of shape functions. Corresponding
formulations have been proposed in the recent literature
predominantly in the context of the isogeometric approach
proposed by Hughes et al. (2005). As standard finite elements
can be regarded as a special case of the isogeometric
approach, there is no need to further distinguish standard
and isogeometric finite elements in most of what follows.
One has to bear in mind, though, that whenever “nodes”
are addressed in the following, these may well be “control
points” in the context of spline-based finite elements.
Apart from the typical step of approximating the function spaces for description of geometry and displacements

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

56

via element-wise shape functions, there are some additional topics arising from the particular nature of models
for thin-walled structures. The most important ones are
approximation of the director field and parametrization of
displacements and rotations. All finite element formulations
described in the literature explicitly or implicitly include
respective assumptions.

7.1

Discretization

procedures of continuum finite elements and does not
include particular technical complications. Approximation
of mid-surface displacements can be accomplished using
C0 -continuous shape functions. For an element with K

Application of membrane elements is restricted to situations in which equilibrium is attainable with membrane
forces only (Section 5.11.1). As there is no bending stiffness, membrane finite elements inherently may include zero
energy deformation modes, which are not rigid-body translations or rotations. For the same reason, equilibrium solutions
including compressive forces (negative normal stresses) are
in general unstable, because the buckling resistance is zero
due to the missing bending stiffness. Realistic membrane
structures, such as tents in civil engineering, are prestressed
and avoid the aforementioned pathologies. Equilibrium is
then – within certain limits – possible for arbitrarily directed
loads due to the activation of geometric stiffness. Including
dynamic effects additionally relaxes the situation due to the
presence of inertial forces.

7.1.2

Kirchhoff–Love elements

Plate and shell finite elements based on Kirchhoff-Love-type
models have for a long time been superseded by
shear-deformable elements with Reissner–Mindlin kinematics. Recently, these elements enjoy a renewed popularity
in applications in which extremely thin structures are considered along with complex deformations, as in the simulation
of parachutes, unfolding of airbags, metal forming, or
crash-worthiness (see, for instance, Çirak et al., 2000 and
references therein).
Similar to the case of membrane elements, the only free
variables are the mid-surface displacements. Formulation of
corresponding finite elements, however, is substantially more
complex due to taking into account curvature and bending.

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

Models and Finite Elements for Thin-Walled Structures

Without explicitly expanding the expressions in equations
(236) and (236), it can be seen that second derivatives of
position vectors R and displacements v show up. In other
words, the variational index of the underlying functional is 2,
necessitating C1 -continuity of the shape functions for geometry and displacements. The complications associated with
this requirement represent a major obstacle for convenient
formulation of plate and shell finite elements based on theories neglecting transverse shear strains (see, for instance,
the sophisticated shell element with bending and derivatives (SHEBA) element by Argyris and Scharpf (1968)).
It is at the same time the major reason for the popularity
of Shear-deformable shell elements, where C0 -continuous
shape functions are sufficient for compatibility, and thus
consistency. Spline-based, isogeometric formulations facilitate the required C1 -continuity of shape functions and thus
have contributed to the renaissance of Kirchhoff–Love shell
theory in computational mechanics (Kiendl et al., 2009).
Still, satisfying continuity conditions in industrial applications, involving coupled spline patches and trimmed geometries, is a challenge (Benson et al., 2013).
For the special case of a linear theory of flat plates, we
can choose the global Cartesian frame {x, y, z} instead of
{𝜉 1 , 𝜉 2 , 𝜉 3 } for parametrization of the structure and obtain

Equation (290) reconfirms the presence of second derivatives in the functional and thus the mathematical requirement
for C1 -continuous shape functions. In many finite element
formulations, this continuity condition is taken into account
using displacement derivatives (rotations of the mid surface)
as nodal degrees of freedom. When using splines with higher
continuity as shape functions, as it is done in the context
of isogeometric finite elements, exclusively displacement
degrees of freedom are needed at the control points. Application of moment loads and clamping boundary conditions
needs special care in this case.
Shape functions are conveniently formulated in natural
element coordinates 𝜉, 𝜂, whereas the continuity requirements have to hold globally, that is, for derivatives with
respect to x and y. The corresponding conditions for the shape
functions, for instance,

for the continuity conditions are extremely complex already
for a simple four-node element and that is why the continuity
conditions in classical plate and shell finite element formulations (as opposed to isogeometric) are exactly satisfied only
for the special cases of rectangular or parallelogram-shaped
elements.
There is a multitude of different triangular and quadrilateral plate and shell elements based on Kirchhoff-Love-type

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

58

models. Even if the mentioned continuity requirements
are not complied with exactly for arbitrary element
shapes, such finite element formulations are called
conforming if nodal degrees of freedom are compatible and the normality condition is identically satisfied
at each point of the plate or shell structure. Often,
such elements exhibit different numbers of degrees of
freedom at corner nodes and edge nodes, which is often
considered unpractical, for instance, concerning data
management, boundary conditions, and connectivity to
other elements. The reader is referred to Zienkiewicz
and Taylor (2000), and references therein, for further
details.
In order to circumvent the difficulty of finding feasible
shape functions and degrees of freedom for conforming
elements, the normality condition can be relaxed. There are
several different methodologies to accomplish this. They
all have in common that the normality condition is satisfied only approximately, for instance, in the context of
a mixed variational formulation. Owing to the nature of
finite element approximations and given a properly designed
method, the resulting error will vanish with mesh refinement.
The substantial advantage of such formulations is the fact
that C0 -continuous shape functions may be used, effectively
eliminating the problems mentioned before in the context of
conforming elements.
Important representatives of this group are the so-called
Discrete Kirchhoff elements, where constraints are applied
at corner nodes and/or edge nodes (see Batoz et al., 1980
for a survey of different formulations). Prior to introduction
of the constraints, the underlying finite element formulation
effectively realizes Reissner–Mindlin kinematics and can be
developed along the lines of Section 7.1.3. An elaborate
treatment of elements using discrete Kirchhoff constraints
can be found in Section 4.18 of Zienkiewicz and Taylor
(2000).
Another possibility to obtain Kirchhoff plate elements
has been presented by Auricchio and Taylor (1994, 1995)
(see also Zienkiewicz et al., 1993; Taylor and Auricchio,
1993). The finite elements described therein are essentially shear deformable. Owing to the special construction
of the underlying variational formulation it is, however,
possible to remove the transverse shear part from the energy
functional without introducing ill-conditioning or numerical
problems, thus switching from a Reissner–Mindlin type to a
Kirchhoff-Love-type formulation. The method is especially
attractive because it combines Kirchhoff elements – for the
thin limit – and locking-free Reissner–Mindlin elements in
one unique formulation (see Section 7.4 for a brief discussion
of “locking” phenomena). The concept has been extended to
shells by Bischoff and Taylor (2001).

59

It is worth a remark at the end of this section that recovery
of stresses and stress resultants for Kirchhoff-Love-type
plate and shell elements does not completely follow the
standard procedure usually employed for structural elements.
The reason is that transverse shear strains are neglected
while transverse shear stresses are necessarily included in
the formulation. As a consequence, transverse shear stress
resultants cannot be obtained directly from the displacements and rotations via kinematic equations and material law but have to be computed using the equilibrium
equations of the shell model. In order to obtain a reasonable representation of transverse shear stress distribution,
one relies on integration of the three-dimensional equilibrium equations.

7.1.3

Reissner–Mindlin elements

Shell finite elements based on the 5-parameter formulation developed throughout Section 5, including transverse
shear strain effects and thus realizing Reissner–Mindlin kinematics, are the most popular ones for finite element analysis. Geometry and displacements of the shell body are
parametrized by independent descriptions of the mid-surface
and director field. As a consequence, C0 -continuity of the
shape functions is sufficient for consistency of the resulting
finite elements.
Position vectors of the mid-surface and the corresponding
director field are usually interpolated by the same shape
functions

Development of displacement-based finite elements
from these expressions is straightforward and not further
elaborated here. For standard Galerkin formulations based
on the virtual work principle, identical shape functions can
be used for interpolation of the mid-surface displacements
and the directors or rotations, respectively. For low-order
elements, Lagrange functions are usually preferred. The
aforementioned problems associated with inter-element
continuity encountered for Kirchhoff–Love elements do not
occur when using Reissner–Mindlin kinematics. However,
locking phenomena described in Section 7.4 represent a
substantial obstacle in the case of thin structures, necessitating additional considerations in element technology for
shear-deformable plate and shell models.
It is interesting at this point to compare once again the
different versions of Reissner–Mindlin (or 5-parameter)
models, following the concepts described in Sections 4.2,

are the same.

crucial point is interpolation of the director or the rotation tensor, respectively. For continuum-based elements, the
director fields in both configurations are naturally obtained
from interpolation of nodal directors, according to equations

61

As a side remark, it may be interesting that the subject
of interpolation of rotations in the context of geometrically exact rods along with until then unbeknown implications on energy conservation, for instance, in the context
of large strain hyperelasticity, has been treated by Crisfield
and Jelenic (1999) (see also Jelenic and Crisfield, 1999)
most recently. They explain the mathematical reasons for the
aforementioned problems and present a corotational interpolation of the rotation field, effectively removing these
deficiencies (see also Romero, 2004 and Computing with
Rotations: Algorithms and Applications for an elaborate
overview of this subject).
When interpolating the director field according to equation
(304), normality of the director ||d|| = 1 only holds at the
nodes. Within the element, its length is not preserved and
||d|| ≤ 1. As a result, the elements tend to behave more
flexibly for curved structures and coarse meshes. As most
finite element formulations generally tend to behave too stiff
for coarse meshes, this is not a major drawback or may
be even an advantage (although the partial cancellation of
two discretization errors in this case is a mere coincidence).
Moreover, the effect is not particularly pronounced.
For the finite element formulation of the geometrically
exact shell theory of Simo and Fox (1989), interpolation
of the director field is proposed in Simo et al. (1989).
The director is normalized at each quadrature point, thus
removing the artificial softening effect mentioned in the
previous paragraph. As a consequence, the mathematical
expressions obtained for the linearization of the director
field are much more involved. In Simo et al. (1989), the
original, simple version is reconsidered. Although violating
unity of the directors at the quadrature points, this leads to
a much simpler formulation, along with acceptable errors
in the range of large rotations but small strains. With mesh
refinement, the condition ||d|| = 1 is gradually recovered.

7.1.4

Higher order elements

K=1 L=1

Obviously, the results obtained by equation (304) and (306)
are not identical.
It has been reported (Başar and Krätzig, 1985; Büchter,
1992; and references therein) that interpolation of rotations
may cause problems for representation of rigid-body rotations, although frame invariance is recovered with mesh
∑Nnod K K
refinement. Note that 𝚲h = K=1
N 𝚲 is in general not
a member of the rotation group, 𝚲h ∉ SO(3), although this
holds for the nodal rotation tensors 𝚲K ∈ SO(3). The reader
is referred to Argyris (1982), Betsch et al. (1998), Botasso
and Borri (1998), Romero (2004), and Computing with
Rotations: Algorithms and Applications for more erudite
discussions of the subject of interpolating rotations.

Finite elements for higher order plate and shell formulations,
as sketched in Section 5.11.4, may be developed along the
same lines as Reissner-Mindlin-type elements. A couple
of details, however, are worth a remark in this context,
mainly due to the reason that finite element discretization in
{𝜉 1 , 𝜉 2 }-direction and semidiscretization in 𝜉 3 -direction are
mutually interactive.
For multi-director models with a single layer, the concept
is relatively clear. Finite elements, for instance, based on
the standard displacement method, can be formulated in
a standard manner. It is important to remark, though, that
refinement of kinematics in the thickness direction necessitates a respectively refined in-plane discretization in order
to resolve the smaller geometric scales associated with the

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

Models and Finite Elements for Thin-Walled Structures

Models and Finite Elements for Thin-Walled Structures

higher order theory (for instance, boundary layers). In this
context, extra requirements need to be mentioned when it
comes to dynamic excitation. The usage of higher order
polynomials in thickness direction can be compared with
a p-version of the finite element method. It does therefore not come as a surprise that finite element formulations
of multi-director plate and shell models often follow this
strategy also in the in-plane direction.
In the context of multi-layer formulations, the situation is
a little more complex. To start with, the notion of a layer for
structural and computational layout in thickness direction is
not uniquely used in the literature. We prefer to distinguish
(Figure 22):
•

Kinematic layer: Each layer has its own kinematic
assumption, for example, having a straight individual
director as in a typical multi-layer shell formulation.
If necessary, these vector fields might be extended to
higher order through-the-thickness interpolations for
each layer.
• Material layer: A material layer has its own constitutive
behavior like a ply in a typical composite or sandwich
structure. This layering may or may not coincide with
the definition of kinematic layers.
• Numerical layer: This addresses numerical preintegration of the constitutive response across the thickness,
that is, the location of sampling points, picking up the
necessary material information. The number of numerical layers (quadrature points) depends on the number of
kinematic and material layers; it is also a consequence of
the evolving material nonlinearities across the thickness
during loading.
Elaborate reviews on multi-layered plates and shells are
given by Carrera (2001, 2002), as well as Vu-Quoc and Tan
(2003a,b). For multi-layer formulations, the same is true
that has been said before in the context of multi-director
elements: refinement in thickness direction prompts in-plane
refinement. Multi-layer plate and shell finite elements have
been mostly developed along with an h-version of the finite
element method, again reflecting the corresponding philosophy for semidiscretization in thickness direction.
As already mentioned in Section 5.11.4, layer-wise plate
and shell finite elements can be interpreted as stacked
standard plate or shell elements. Assuming a layer-wise
5-parameter model, this means that three degrees of freedom
are needed for the in-plane displacement plus two independent rotation parameters for each layer. For a layer-wise
7-parameter model, this makes three plus number of layers
times four degrees of freedom. It is easily perceivable that
this leads to a high number of nodal degrees of freedom for
structures with a large number of layers.

Alternative concepts have therefore been developed, in
which not every individual layer has its own degree of
freedom, but a globally defined zig–zag function across the
thickness is applied, associated with one or two degrees of
freedom only (Carrera, 1996; Aitharaju and Averill, 1999).
Such formulations, however, are restricted to situations in
which a certain periodicity of displacement distributions in
thickness direction can be anticipated and corresponding
finite elements did not gain significant practical importance.

7.2

Definition of shell director

It is obvious that the properties of a shell finite element
formulation strongly depend on the underlying shell theory.
However, at the same time, the choice of a specific finite
element may involve a modeling assumption in its own right
for the discretized system. While any given finite element
formulation of a specific shell theory eventually produces
identical results as h → 0, provided it is consistent, considerable differences may be observed for finite meshes. As this
reflects the actual case of practical significance, it is important to know about the characteristics of the most prominent
techniques to develop shell finite elements. Moreover, it is
important to distinguish between these effects and the ones
previously discussed in Section 5.
One of the most important differences between different
shell elements described in the literature and available in
commercial codes concerns definition of the shell director.
Let us first assume that a normalized director is used in the
reference configuration, ||D|| = 1. Shell theories emanating
from derivation from three-dimensional continuum (Section
4.2) as well as the direct approach (Section 4.3) realize this
concept. Shell finite elements developed on the basis of the
degenerated solid approach (Section 4.4) naturally employ
||DK || = t∕2. However, normalization is possible and does
not introduce additional difficulties (see equation (129)).
It will be of some importance in this section that the
director, owing to its name, defines the thickness direction of
the shell. Consequently, the way in which a two-dimensional
shell element extrudes to a three-dimensional solid crucially
depends on the definition of the director. This inherently
contains the direction of preintegration of the material law.
The substantial questions in the context of definition of the
director are whether
•
•

one single, common director at each node, or a set of
element-wise defined directors is used and
the director is orthogonal to the mid-surface of the shell
element or not.

See Figure 21 for an illustration of corresponding situations. Answering both questions with “yes” generates the

See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

requirement of C1 -continuity of the discretized mid-surface
of the shell, which is usually avoided. Consequently, a
compromise in one or the other way has to be accepted.
When using common directors at nodes (Ahmad et al.,
1968; Ahmad et al., 1970; Ramm, 1976; Vu-Quoc and Mora,
1989), thus giving up the requirement of DK being orthogonal
to the mid-surface of the shell, the question of its definition
arises. A natural choice is the normalized algebraic average
of all nodal directors DK(e) of the adjacent elements,
Ne
∑

DK(e)

‖∑

(307)

Using common directors has a couple of advantages. First,
all interelement continuity conditions are automatically satisfied. Second, DK can be utilized to establish unique local
director coordinate systems at each node, which is beneficial
for the parametrization of rotations (Section 7.3). Finally, as
we will see, it may avoid the necessity to introduce drilling
degrees of freedom.
These advantages are compromised by the fact that DK will
in general not be orthogonal to the discretized mid-surface of
the shell. As a consequence, the strain variables derived upon
this premise are theoretically incomplete. This has already
been discussed previously when comparing the degenerated
solid approach and shell theory. The resulting error is usually
considered small and vanishes with mesh refinement.
The main problem, however, is the treatment of intersections. There is no concept known to the authors that satisfactorily defines a unique director at points where three or
more shell surfaces meet in arbitrary angles. Apart from the
already mentioned fact of violation of the normality condition, there is also an unavoidable overlapping of material. In

Figure 22, the two-dimensional equivalent of such a situation
is shown. The method of using individual element directors,
an average, nodal director, and a discretization with solid
elements are compared. Apart from the problem of overlapping material, which is also present when using individual
directors, the strong violation of the orthogonality becomes
apparent. The reason for the problem becomes apparent
when looking at the three-dimensional discretization with
solid elements (2-D in this model problem). The connecting
element within the joint does not have a distinct mid-surface
and a corresponding discretization with shell elements must
necessarily lead to contradictions.
Especially in the context of three-dimensional shell
formulations, using difference vectors instead of rotations, normalization of the director is omitted, allowing for
varying shell thickness within one element. In this case,
definition of an average director can lead to an artificial
necking at discrete kinks or intersections and thus a weakening of the structure. It is then favorable to define the
director according to the actual three-dimensional geometry
(Bischoff, 1999).
From a strictly mathematical point of view, such situations
are excluded a priori because they violate certain smoothness
conditions, required for validity of shell theories. Note that
at a kink, curvature is infinite and the corresponding tangent
spaces are not well defined. For the case of kinks emanating
from discretization, this is not a problem, because they vanish
with mesh refinement and the discretized tangent spaces are
readily available.
Although ignored in many papers on shell theories and
related elements, assuming sufficient smoothness from the
beginning, this problem is of utmost importance in practical
applications, which is why shell finite elements in commercial codes usually define element-wise directors that are
exactly orthogonal to the discretized mid-surface of the shell.
In this case, all conditions used while deriving the shell
theories in Section 5 are met within each element. Moreover,

Figure 22. Shells with intersections.

the procedure of finding an average director at each node is
avoided.
As already indicated, the main motivation for not using
common directors is probably the treatment of shells with
kinks and intersections. Figure 22 demonstrates the advantage of defining individual directors in such cases.
For the same reason – namely to facilitate modeling
of kinks and intersections – drilling rotations are usually
included when using individual directors. Figure 23 depicts
the situation of two flat plates intersecting at a right angle.
Coupling of rotations with respect to the x-axis is trivial.
For rotations with respect to y- and z-directions, however,
the situation is different. What leads to twist in one plate
represents a drilling rotation for the other, and vice versa.
As there is no immediately obvious concept available for the
definition of stiffnesses associated with the drilling rotations

𝜉y(1) , 𝜉z(2) (we will elaborate a little more on this topic in the
subsequent section), the question arises how this coupling
could be accomplished. One possibility is to simply omit
the coupling, thus modeling the intersection a little too
flexibly. Corresponding elements are thus not consistent,
because they violate the compatibility condition. Experience, however, implies that convergence is retained anyway.
When coupling the corresponding degrees of freedom, model
behavior depends on the definition of the drilling stiffness.
Convergence to the same result is then subject to conditions
that have to be fulfilled by the definition of drilling stiffness. To sum up, one can say that for smooth shells using an
average director is the most appealing concept. In the context
of complex problems involving kinks and intersections, definition of individual directors along with the introduction of
drilling rotations is probably the better choice.

64

7.3

Parametrization of displacements and
rotations

Models for thin-walled structures employ dimensional
reduction to express the three-dimensional displacement
field by quantities that are defined on a two-dimensional
domain. This section deals with the question, which
discrete quantities are those that are in the context of
finite element discretizations. For Kirchhoff–Love and
Reissner-Mindlin-type shell finite elements, the nodal
degrees of freedom are displacements and rotations.
For three-dimensional shell elements, often difference
displacements instead of rotations are used. The so-called
surface-oriented or solid-shell finite elements are essentially
three-dimensional discretizations with displacement degrees
of freedom at the upper and lower surfaces of the shell.
As already implied in Section 5.11, formulation of
membrane finite elements only requires discretization of the
mid-surface displacements v. Geometry of reference and
current configurations are uniquely defined by the position
vectors to points on the mid-surface R and r. Neither a
director nor a rotation tensor is needed. Moreover, the
weak form does not contain second-order derivatives of
the displacements, because bending moments are excluded.
Thus, standard C0 -continuous shape functions can be used.
There is virtually no complication or peculiarity involved
and parametrization is usually accomplished by simply
defining nodal displacements and position vectors with
respect to a global Cartesian frame.
In the case of Kirchhoff-Love-type shell elements, additionally bending moments and transverse shear forces are
considered. The weak form contains second-order derivatives thus leading to the requirement of C1 -continuity for the
shape functions across interelement boundaries. The associated complexity in derivation of suitable shape functions for
arbitrarily shaped elements was one of the main motivations
for the further development of shear-deformable shell finite
elements.
It is a typical feature of many Kirchhoff-Love-type plate
and shell elements that different sets of degrees of freedom
are used at corner nodes and edge nodes; see, for instance,
Morley (1991). Some of these elements are fairly efficient
and widely used in science and practice. Typical degrees of
freedom of such elements are – apart from the usual nodal
displacements – displacement derivatives, representing rotations or twisting deformations. Classical 5-parameter formulations utilize the aforementioned three components of the
mid-surface displacements plus two independent rotations.
For linearized theories, the definition of practical rotation
parameters is not particularly difficult.
One of the major questions, especially in the context of
shell elements, concerns the introduction of drilling rotations

65

(see, for instance, Ibrahimbegovic, 1994). There are two
major reasons why drilling degrees of freedom are desirable:
1.

2.

the treatment of shells with kinks and intersection as well
as coupling of shell elements with trusses and beams, for
instance, to model its connection to columns or stiffeners
and
reasons of element technology, trying to improve the
in-plane behavior, particularly for triangular elements.

In the continuous theory, independent drilling rotations do
not exist. The rotation of a material point on the mid-surface
about the normal can be computed from the in-plane
displacement field. If independent drilling rotations are
artificially introduced, the questions of appropriate stiffness,
energetically conjugate moments, and feasible boundary
conditions arise. One possibility is to simply suppress the
drilling degree of freedom at nodes where it is not needed
(Hughes and Liu, 1981). This requires the parametrization
of rotations in local coordinate systems. An alternative
possibility, avoiding the necessity to define local coordinate
systems, is a penalty formulation or mixed method where
the corresponding condition is symmetry of the membrane
stresses (Sansour and Bufler, 1992; Büchter, 1992). In
either case, interpolation of the mid-surface displacement is
independent of the drilling rotation.
This is different for formulations of the second group,
introducing drilling rotations in the context of parametrization of in-plane displacements, as depicted in Figure 24
(Allmann, 1984, 1994). The drilling rotation is in this case
related to the corresponding in-plane displacements and
consequently the stiffness depends on the element size. The
motivation for introduction of such degrees of freedom originates from the need to enhance the function spaces for
the displacements in order to avoid in-plane shear locking
and volumetric locking and it is particularly popular for the
formulation of triangular elements. Corresponding concepts
have been developed in the context of two-dimensional solid
elements, and their implications on related shell elements are
merely a side effect.
In the limit h → 0, the drilling stiffness is zero as the corresponding deformation localizes in the elements adjacent to
the corresponding node. This is consistent to the theory but
it means that utilizing such degrees of freedom for connection to physical rotational degrees of freedom of beam and
shell elements is not recommendable.
If independent rotations around two axes, orthogonal to
the director, are used, drilling rotations are automatically
excluded (see, for instance, Zienkiewicz and Taylor, 2000 for
a simple method to uniquely obtain such a system). If fixed
axes are used, a singularity is encountered when the director
is collinear to one of those axes because the corresponding

A crucial role in the parametrization of rotations is due to
the definition of the director, as discussed in the previous
section. We shall assume in the following that one unique
director at each node is available. As the special case of
infinitesimal rotations is contained in the more general theory
of large rotations, we shall not deal with further mathematical details here and proceed to the discussion of the
nonlinear case.
Parametrization of large rotations represents a broad and
mathematically demanding field. Until today, substantial
contributions realizing considerable improvements in the
field of large rotation beam and shell finite elements are being
published. In this issue of the Encyclopedia of Computational Mechanics, the chapter Computing with Rotations:
Algorithms and Applications is devoted to the subject of
treating rotations, not only in the context of thin-walled structures.

The first condition in SD2 simply states that 𝚯 is the rotation
vector belonging to 𝚲. The second condition ensures its
orthogonality to the director vector D. A drilling rotation is
thus explicitly excluded.
As the nine components of 𝚲 have to fulfill six conditions (equation (309), conservation of three lengths and three
angles), three independent parameters remain to uniquely
specify a rotation in ℝ3 . This means that, for a given 𝚲 ∈
SO(3), the map
𝚲 ∶ {A1 , A2 , A3 } → {a1 , a2 , a3 } = 𝚲 ⋅ {A1 , A2 , A3 } (314)
is uniquely defined. For a given pair of vectors D, d (instead
of complete triads), uniqueness is lost, that is, there is an
infinite number of rotation tensors 𝚲 for which d = 𝚲 ⋅ D
holds. The origin of this issue will be elaborated in what
follows.
First, a rotation 𝚲 with respect to an axis that is collinear
to some vector 𝚯 leads to the identity 𝚲 ⋅ 𝚯 = 𝚯. Thus, 𝚯
is an eigenvector of 𝚲 with eigenvalue 1. The existence of
this eigenvector, called the rotation vector, is inherent to
members of SO(3). The rotation vector will later on prove
useful for the development of closed form expressions for
𝚲. In the present context, however, we record that from
d = 𝚲 ⋅ D and d = D, it does not follow that 𝚲 = G, that
is, the identity map. On the contrary, an infinite number
of second-order tensors {𝚲 ∈ SO(3) | 𝚲 ⋅ d = D} does exist.
Geometrically, this is the set of rotations about an axis

parallel to D, usually called drilling rotations, given by
SO(3)∖SD2 .
As a consequence, even if d ≠ D, the corresponding 𝚲
may not be uniquely obtained. In order to envision this fact,
let 𝚲D ∈ SO(3)∖SD2 be the aforementioned rotation such that
𝚲D ⋅ D = D. We may now express the mapping in question as d = 𝚲 ⋅ D = 𝚲 ⋅ 𝚲D ⋅ D. Obviously, 𝚲D ⋅ 𝚲 ≠ 𝚲 in
general and we conclude that the corresponding mapping is
nonunique.
For shell theories, this means that a rotation around the
director vector acts like an identity map and is thus associated
with zero energy. Consequently, the corresponding structural
stiffness is zero. Further issues concerning nonuniqueness
and singularities are covered later on, because those problems depend on the parametrization of 𝚲 as well as the
nonlinear solution procedure and are not inherent to members
of SO(3) a priori.
Out of the numerous different possibilities for practical
implementation of 𝚲, we choose its representation with the
help of a rotation vector. Owing to the mathematical structure of the rotation group, concepts described in the literature
are often very abstract. Within the mathematical framework
provided by the theory of manifolds, the use of a rotation
vector is neither necessary nor particularly useful. We will,
however, see that starting from the simple and very descriptive notion of a rotation axis along with a rotation angle, it
is possible to eventually arrive at the same singularity-free
algorithms provided by mathematically more sophisticated

This means that in either case 𝚲 still includes the previously
discussed drilling rotation, mapping a vector that is collinear
to the axes of rotation onto itself.
In the case of shells, the corresponding “deformation”
is not associated with a stiffness. More precisely, rotation of a material point about the director axis is already
uniquely described by the in-plane displacement field (it can
be obtained from the polar decomposition of the deformation gradient in the tangent plane). A corresponding drilling
moment does not exist in a nonpolar continuum (note that the
lever arm of the related couple would be zero).
A concrete implementation of a rotation vector 𝚯 ∈ TD S2
can, for instance, be achieved by
𝚯 = Θ1 A1 + Θ2 A2

(326)

where A1 and A2 are the covariant base vectors of the
mid-surface of the shell, which are by construction orthogonal to D. This is probably not the most elegant or efficient
possibility (see Simo and Fox, 1989 for alternatives) but
is conceptually simple and straightforward in the present
context. In the case of nonorthogonal director fields, special
tangent vectors may be employed.
A unique parameterization of 𝚲 ∈ SD2 may now be accomplished with two independent variables, the third being
subject to the given subsidiary condition.

convergence problems may be encountered.
Methods to overcome the second problem are described in
Section 7.5 because they are related to solution methods. To
be more precise, a multiplicative update of the rotation tensor
in the context of an incremental-iterative solution procedure
removes this trouble. A multiplicative structure appears to be
the first choice anyway when approaching the problem from
the mathematical side because, as already mentioned, the
rotation group is not a linear space and the natural connection
of a series of rotations is multiplication instead of addition.

Locking phenomena and finite element
technology

(328)

This expression is equivalent to applying the Rodrigues
formula, equation (320), along with d = 𝚲 ⋅ D if it is ensured
that the rotation vector is orthogonal to D.
We close this brief discussion of large rotations with some
remarks on singularities. Let us consider a given rotation
tensor 𝚲, along with the corresponding rotation vector 𝚯,
and assume that the latter is orthogonal to D, that is, drilling
rotations are excluded. The mapping of D onto d is well
defined, however, its inverse is not unique for arbitrary cases.
The reason for this fact is the impossibility of creating a
one-to-one two-dimensional map of a sphere; take the lines

Locking phenomena and finite element technology represent
individual, vast fields of scientific interest in their own right
and they cannot be treated thoroughly in the context of this
overview. We will, however, try to shed some light on the
most important issues in the context of plate and shell finite
elements.
Mathematically, the reason for locking is an illconditioning, typical for stiff differential equations. For
shear-deformable plates and shells, the ratio of transverse
shear stiffness to bending stiffness tends to infinity in the
thin limit. Even small discretization errors in the transverse
shear term therefore lead to unacceptably large global errors.

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

Models and Finite Elements for Thin-Walled Structures

Models and Finite Elements for Thin-Walled Structures

This phenomenon is called transverse shear locking in the
literature. Similar mechanisms lead to membrane locking,
being related to the membrane stiffness and curvature thickness locking (or trapezoidal locking), in the case of shell
elements including transverse normal strains. In the latter
case, the ratio of transverse normal stiffness and bending
stiffness tends to infinity in the thin limit.
Mechanically, locking can be explained by the inability of
a finite element formulation to represent certain deformation
modes without unwanted, parasitic strains and/or stresses.
Thus, plate and shell elements suffering from transverse
shear locking are unable to represent pure bending without
parasitic shear strains. Membrane locking manifests itself
in the presence of parasitic membrane strains in the case of
inextensional (pure bending) deformations. As for thin plates
and shells, the stiffness activated by the parasitic strains is
extremely large compared to bending stiffness, the resulting
deformations are severely underestimated.
It is important to remark that these locking phenomena
are not a result of dimensional reduction or application
of particular plate or shell models, but they are inherent
in the physical problem and automatically transferred to a
certain class of finite element discretizations for thin-walled
structures. On the contrary, specific plate or shell models
may be free from certain locking effects because they
neglect related mechanical phenomena (for instance, Kirchhoff plate elements do not suffer from transverse shear
locking; curvature thickness locking is not present in
conventional 5-parameter or Reissner–Mindlin elements,
neglecting the effect of transverse normal strains). When
using three-dimensional elements for the analysis of plates
and shells, all locking effects are there with exactly the same
implications on accuracy, efficiency, and element technology
as for plate and shell elements.

7.4.1

Transverse shear locking

Transverse shear locking can occur in shear-deformable
plate and shell finite elements, that is, elements based on
Reissner–Mindlin kinematics, or higher order theories. The
artificial stiffening effect depends on the slenderness of the
element (ratio of element length to thickness) and becomes
more pronounced as the element gets thinner. The effect is
so strong that computation of thin plates and shells with
linear standard displacement elements is practically impossible. Especially, transverse shear forces are subject to large
errors; a typical symptom are strong oscillations.
For second and higher order elements, as well as
spline-based (isogeometric) Elements, the displacements do
not suffer as much as when using linear shape functions.
However, the oscillations still spoil the solution.

The mechanical reason for this phenomenon are parasitic transverse shear forces, particularly pronounced in pure
bending situations. They can be explained with the simple
example of a linear Timoshenko beam element, taking into
account transverse shear deformations.
The kinematic equation for the linearized transverse shear
strains reads

apparently converges much faster, the considerable underestimation of the displacements by Q1, even for relatively fine
meshes, can be observed. Note that with the given slenderness of (L∕t) = 20, the plate is not extremely thin.

the center deflection obtained with a mesh of 4 × 4 elements,
to the exact solution is plotted versus slenderness in a log–log
scale. It can be seen that for both elements, the error depends
on slenderness L∕t. However, the situation is less dramatic
for Q2 than for Q1.
Formulations to avoid transverse shear locking in plate
finite elements – most of them being directly transferable to
shells – have been developed since the early days of finite
element analysis. Some important concepts are listed in the
following:

•

•

•

•

•

Reduced integration, possibly along with hourglass control to avoid artificial zero energy modes
(Zienkiewicz et al., 1973; Hughes, 1987; Zienkiewicz
and Taylor, 2000; Belytschko et al., 2014).
Assumed strain methods, employing specific interpolations of transverse shear strains, pioneered by MacNeal
(1978), Hughes and Tezduyar (1981), and later on
systematically extended to the family of MITC elements
by Bathe and coworkers (see, for instance, Bathe and
Dvorkin, 1985, 1986; Bathe et al., 1989a,b). Quadratic
assumed strain elements have been proposed by Huang
and Hinton (1984), Park and Stanley (1986), and Pinsky
and Jang (1987), among others.
B-bar methods, such as assumed strain methods modifying the strain displacement matrix (B-operator matrix)
in order to get rid of parasitic transverse shear strains
(Hughes and Liu, 1981). The discrete strain gap (DSG)
method, proposed by Bletzinger et al. (2000) (see also
Bischoff and Bletzinger, 2001; Koschnick et al., 2004),
can also be attributed to this group.
Elements based on multifield variational formulations,
like the EAS method (Simo and Rifai, 1990). Particularly attractive representatives of this group appear to be
elements based on “linked interpolation”, as described
in Tessler and Hughes (1985), Zienkiewicz et al. (1993),
Taylor and Auricchio (1993), and Bischoff and Taylor
(2001).
Alternative parametrization of displacements and rotations using discretizations with higher continuity (Long
et al., 2012; Echter et al., 2013). Within the still new technique of isogeometric analysis it is thus possible to avoid

Membrane locking

Although literature in finite element technology in general
is extremely rich, the problem of membrane locking has
enjoyed less attention than, for instance, transverse shear
locking in plates and shells. One reason might be that when
using bilinear shape functions, probably the most popular
basis for shell finite elements, the effect is relatively mild,
or not present at all in particular cases. Linear triangular
elements are completely free from membrane locking.
Moreover, membrane locking is hard to tackle. It only
occurs in the case of curved elements and – in contrast to
most other locking problems – it is therefore difficult to
investigate with the help of simple toy examples, involving
only regular (nondistorted) elements. In a lot of cases,
methods that have originally been developed in the context
of solid elements with the aim of avoiding in-plane shear
locking or volumetric locking have later on been transferred
to the analysis of shells to alleviate membrane locking (see,
for instance, Andelfinger and Ramm, 1993).
Avoiding membrane locking, however, is as important as
avoiding shear locking in order to obtain sensible results
with reasonable numerical effort, especially when quadratic

with a shallow beam (or shell) formulation. In general situations, that is, deep shell theory, this is nonzero as well.
Again, a simple model problem is used to demonstrate
behavior of standard displacement finite elements. We
consider a 90∘ section of a cylindrical shell under pure
bending, as illustrated in Figure 28. Setting Poisson’s ratio
to zero, 𝜈 = 0, generates a uniaxial bending problem of a
curved beam. Mesh refinement in hoop direction only is
therefore sufficient for convergence to the exact solution.
The diagram in Figure 28 reveals that in contrast to the plate
example of the previous section, the biquadratic element Q2
also does not behave satisfactorily in this case. The severe
lack of accuracy compared to the pure plate bending situation
before is caused by membrane locking. The bilinear element
Q1 is effectively free from membrane locking in this case,
because the individual elements are flat. The bad behavior
in this example is only due to transverse shear locking.
Obviously, uniform convergence in t can be expected for
neither of those elements.

improves the situation but does not completely alleviate
membrane locking. The problem is, however, reduced to an
amount that the remaining deficiencies are merely academic.
Within a range of thickness that is of practical interest,
these elements are practically free from membrane locking.
Again, we refer to Zienkiewicz and Taylor (2000) for further
references.

7.4.3

Curvature thickness locking (trapezoidal
locking)

Similar to membrane locking, curvature thickness locking
exclusively occurs for curved elements. The origin of
curvature thickness locking are parasitic transverse normal
strains, which is why this phenomenon only occurs in
three-dimensional (7-parameter and higher order) models.
Conventional 5-parameter formulations are free from
curvature thickness locking.
The problem has been reported first by Ramm et al.
(1994, 1995), and Betsch et al. (1995) in the context of
7-parameter shell elements. However, a similar effect for
two-dimensional and three-dimensional solid elements
termed trapezoidal locking has been long known (see,
MacNeal, 1994; Sze, 2000 and references therein).
In the context of shell elements, curvature thickness
locking can be removed by an assumed strain interpolation
of the constant part of the transverse normal strains using one
sampling point in the center of the element, as proposed by
Ramm et al. (1994, 1995). This procedure is in fact identical
to selective reduced integration of this strain component.
Assumed strain interpolations using the nodes as sampling
points have been presented by Betsch et al. (1995) and
Bischoff and Ramm (1997).

7.5

Solution methods

In this section, implications of specific properties of
thin-walled structures and corresponding finite element
formulations on solution methods are discussed. We assume
that the reader is familiar with the basics of the underlying
concepts and focus on the peculiarities that might come
into play when dealing with beam, plate, and shell finite
elements.
In the most simple case of a linearized theory, we are
dealing with a linear system of algebraic equations. Both
direct and iterative solvers are applied to solve this problem
numerically. In both cases, conditioning of the stiffness

matrix has a certain impact on the quality of the solution and
the rate of convergence when iterative solvers are used.
For the same reasons discussed in the context of locking
problems, stiffness matrices of plate and shell elements
usually exhibit high condition numbers. As the condition
number is defined as the ratio of the highest to the lowest
eigenvalue, and the eigenvalues, in turn, represent stiffness
of the corresponding deformation mode, the reason is quite
clear: as thickness decreases, the ratio of membrane and
shear stiffness to bending stiffness increases, and so does the
condition number.
When applying direct solvers, the condition number may
have an impact on the number of digits lost because of
round-off errors. Given the number of digits used in modern
finite element codes, this problem is not as important any
more as it used to be. The situation is different when iterative solvers are applied. In this case, the number of iterations
needed for the error to fall below a certain threshold crucially
depends on the condition number. Here, feasible precondition procedures may prove helpful, if not indispensable.
In three-dimensional shell models including thickness strains, the situation is worse than in conventional
5-parameter plate and shell elements. The reason is the additional effect of transverse normal stiffness. Simo et al. (1989)
solve this problem by decoupling bending and transverse
normal strain effects, utilizing a formulation with a rotation
tensor and an additional scalar parameter for describing
thickness change. An alternative method, preserving the
difference vector formulation of the 7-parameter model
mentioned in Section 5 and thus circumventing the involved
mathematics of a formulation using rotations, has been
proposed by Gee et al. (2003). Within this concept, a simple
scaling of the director effectively eliminates the additional
effect of transverse normal strains on the condition number,
thus retaining the typical behavior of a standard 5-parameter
model. In the context of explicit dynamics, the stiffest deformation modes determines the critical time step size. In this
case, the aforementioned scaling may have a tremendous
effect on computational efficiency. A similar result may be
obtained by the selective mass scaling technique proposed
by Cocchetti et al. (2014) for solid-shell elements.
For nonlinear static problems (cf. Section 6), efficient
algorithms for tracing the equilibrium path are crucial.
Standard load-controlled Newton–Raphson schemes are
only successful in the case of strictly monotonic load–
displacement curves. In general situations, arc length
methods have to be applied. For an overview of the most
popular and efficient methods, see Belytschko et al. (2014),
Crisfield (1995) and Buckling.
Both for simple load control and highly sophisticated arc
length methods, a nonlinear system of algebraic equations
has to be solved. In any case, a parameter s is involved,

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

parametrizing the equilibrium path. In the simplest case
of load control, s is identical to the load factor 𝜆. The
typical procedure to approximate a load–displacement path
consists in gradually increasing s by distinct increments,
solving the resulting nonlinear sub-problems iteratively.
Within this incremental-iterative procedure, the state variables, particularly the primary unknowns, have to be updated.
For the displacements, being members of a linear vector
space, an additive update is the natural choice. In the case
of rotations, the situation is a bit more complex.

where TOL is a small number whose sensible choice is
problem dependent.
The procedure described so far is quite standard. The fact
that the unknown parameters are stored separately in two
vectors dv and d𝜃 has not yet been exploited – both vectors
are treated identically.
The involved additive update of the rotation parameters
(viz. the rotation vector), however, has some drawbacks once
finite rotations are considered. In Section 7.3, nonuniqueness
of the representation of rotations by 180∘ via a rotation vector
has been discussed. The reason was identified as the fact that
any rotation vector that is perpendicular to the director would
realize this rotation, thus allowing for an infinite number of
solutions to (348). This may result in numerical instabilities
for rotations close to 180∘ .
One possibility of relaxing the situation is to update the
director after each increment, that is, defining the director
of the last converged deformation state as the reference
director (like in an updated Lagrange formulation). Arbitrary
rotations can then be described as long as one individual
increment does not reach 180∘ . A more elegant procedure
is to update the director within each iteration. In this case,
the aforementioned problem is completely eliminated and the
numerical procedure is stable for arbitrary rotation angles. It
is not trivial to prove this fact; however, one might accept
the following heuristic explanation. The most critical case
is obviously an angle of 180∘ . The first step in the iteration
cannot cause any problem because it constitutes a linearized
solution. Thus, it is excluded that the predictor “accidentally”
exactly hits the critical case d = −D. If we assume that the
procedure converges monotonically, all subsequent corrector
steps will be smaller than 180∘ . As the reference director

throughout the entire procedure.

Analogously to equation (348), the definition Πh ̂ ∶=
i,𝜃
𝜕Πhi ∕𝜕 d̂ 𝜃 has been used. The most remarkable difference is
that a multiplicative update of the rotation tensor instead
of an additive update of the rotation vector is performed.
Mathematically, this multiplicative structure is a direct
consequence of properties of the underlying spaces. For
the mid-surface displacements dv being members of the
linear vector space ℝ3 , an additive update structure is quite
natural. The rotation group SO(3), however, containing the
rotation tensor 𝚲h , is not a linear space and for any two 𝚲1h
and 𝚲2h , we have in general 𝚲1h + 𝚲2h ∉ SO(3). The detour of

expressing 𝚲h in terms of a rotation vector 𝜽, which, in turn,
could be updated additively appears to be a pitfall. It might
be called a historical accident, emanating from the classical
concepts to parameterize rotations via rotation angles with
respect to fixed or follower axes.
However, derivation of the multiplicative update procedure
on the basis of the considerations elaborated above sheds
some light on the mechanical and geometrical significance
of the quantities involved. As already mentioned, the values
contained in d̂ 𝜃 are angles of rotation, updating the director
from the last iteration to the current state. This means that
those values will eventually fall below the convergence tolerance and have no significance in describing the current state
in comparison to the reference configuration. It is therefore not quite correct to view the iteration process using
a multiplicative update as the solution of a nonlinear set
of equations with displacement and rotational degrees of
freedom as unknowns. Instead of rotations, the unknown is
merely the current state, represented by either the rotation
tensor or the director vector itself. In fact, many works on
treatment of large rotations directly start from this philosophy; see, for instance, Ramm (1976, 1977).
The procedure given above should not be viewed as “the”
multiplicative update procedure. In fact there are many variants, distinguished mainly by different parametrizations of
the rotations. In some cases, this involves nonsymmetric
tangent operators during iteration. The severe disadvantages coming along can be avoided by symmetrizing the
tangent, remarkably without harming quadratic convergence,
as shown by Simo and Vu-Quoc (1986a).

8

CONCLUDING REMARKS

Thin-walled structures have their peculiar mechanical
behavior: high in-plane and low out-of-plane resistance,
supplemented by high transverse shear and transverse
normal stiffness, once these effects are also considered.
These are physical phenomena and a cause for many
structural sensitivities. It is self-evident that these built-in
characteristics automatically transfer to any mechanical
model, discretization scheme, and numerical procedure.
Typical examples are locking phenomena in which deficiencies in low-order formulations are magnified in a
nonacceptable way. Furthermore, ill-conditioning leading to
potential failure of iterative solvers and severe consequences
in explicit dynamics belongs to this area.
Owing to increasing computer capacity on the one hand
and higher demands in practice on the other hand, a strong
indication to “go three-dimensional” may be observed in the
past two decades. In other words, even thin-walled structures
are simulated by three-dimensional solid-shell elements,

10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026, Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119176817.ecm2026 by Technical University Delft, Wiley Online Library on [13/11/2026]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

76

allowing direct implementation of three-dimensional constitutive models as well as inclusion of higher order effects
not represented by classical theories for thin-walled structures. Typical examples are boundary layer effects, delamination, load inflow from surfaces, and transition to compact
solid parts. It is a trivial remark that the mentioned principal character of the thin structure with all its sensitivities
is not relaxed by a three-dimensional analysis method; it
might even become more complex, for example, showing
singularities in a linear-elastic analysis not seen in a classical
two-dimensional model. The classification of the increase of
model order across the thickness as a higher order plate or
shell formulation or as an increase of the discretization order
in thickness direction (either by a multi-layer concept or the
p-version) is a matter of taste.
Recently, the discussion about the superiority of low-order
or high-order finite elements for thin-walled structures has
been enriched by the possibility of using spline-based finite
elements within the isogeometric approach. It is in particular the potential to construct function spaces with higher
continuity that opens up new possibilities, such as relatively
simple formulation of Kirchhoff-Love-type shell elements
or rotation-free representation of shear-deformable plate and
shell models. The potential shift of paradigms emanating
from such developments demonstrates that the end of the
evolution of this classical discipline is yet out of sight.

Note that we have also made use of the symmetry of E and E,
respectively. With equations (110) and (A17) we can write
E ∶ S = Z−1 ⋅ Ê ⋅ Z−T ∶ ZT ⋅ Ŝ ⋅ Z

As this is a scalar equation, left and right multiplying with a

Hence

(A21)

concludes the proof. The fact that Ŝ and Ê are energetically
conjugate is an immediate consequence of this result.

(A27)

In the given context, it had been assumed that the initial
shell thickness t is constant with respect to 𝜉 𝛼 and a3 is
obtained from a rotation of A3 . Consequently, ||a3 || and ||A3 ||
are constant with respect to 𝜉 𝛼 as well. For the proof of (A27),
we may assume without loss of generality that A3 ∈ S2 , that
is, it is a unit vector (this corresponds to dividing equation
(A27) by t2 ). From inextensibility of the director, it follows
that a3 ∈ S2 as well. Moreover, it is obviously sufficient –
though not necessary – to prove that
a3,𝛼 ⋅ a3 = 0

As the arguments that follow apply to both reference and

The coordinates 𝜉 𝛼 represent a parametrization of the unit
sphere S2 , which is singularity-free in the vicinity of a3 . The
partial derivatives a3,1 and a3,2 are thus linearly independent
vectors in the tangent plane,
a3,𝛼 ∈ Ta3 S2

(A29)

which is the set of vectors orthogonal to a3 , from which the
assertion follows.

about angles dΘ1 , dΘ2 , via a3,𝛼 as
d𝚲𝜀 ⋅ a3 = a3 + a3,𝛼 dΘ𝛼

Finally, it is of course possible to simply express a3 and its
derivative in terms of an arbitrary reference vector in S2 and
a rotation tensor, for instance,
a 3 = 𝚲 ⋅ e3 ,

a3,𝛼 = 𝚲,𝛼 ⋅ e3

(A33)

Using any representation of 𝚲, for instance, equation (320),
leaves us with the task of verifying that
(𝚲,𝛼 ⋅ e3 ) ⋅ (𝚲 ⋅ e3 ) = 0

(A34)

If Cartesian coordinates are used for the components of 𝚲,
this reduces to checking whether the inner product of the
third columns (because we used e3 ) of the corresponding
coefficient matrices vanishes. Nevertheless, the resulting
expressions are somewhat lengthy and we refrain from reproducing this in detail. The procedure is, however, well suited
to verify the validity of (A28), for instance, with the help of
computer algebra systems.
The argument can be easily extended to shells with
non-constant thickness. To this end, we equation (A27) is
rewritten as
(t a3 ),𝛼 ⋅ t a3 − (t A3 ),𝛼 ⋅ t A3 = 0

(A35)

t,𝛼 a3 ⋅ a3 t + t a3,𝛼 ⋅ a3 t − t,𝛼 A3 ⋅ A3 t − t A3,𝛼 ⋅ A3 t = 0
(A36)
Introducing the relations from equation (A28), which have
already been proven, and realizing that a3 ⋅ a3 = A3 ⋅ A3 =
1 leads to
t,𝛼 t − t,𝛼 t = 0
(A37)
concluding the proof for the case of non-constant thickness.
It is interesting to remark that the linear part of the transverse shear strains does not vanish in the context of the
degenerated solid approach, cf. Section 4.4, equation (151).
The reason is that interpolation of nodal directors does not
preserve their respective lengths, thus removing a substantial
prerequisite for the corresponding terms to vanish.
Components of material tensor for orthotropic material
For orthotropic material laws, the number of independent
coefficients Cijkl of C reduces from 21 to 9. Note that the
corresponding indices then refer to the characteristic directions of the material, which in general do not coincide with
the coordinate lines of the shell. The individual components
are given as
